- 2026-04-05T20:29 Mat_Tr https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/18 ::  I think I’ve figured out the reason why the limits are being consumed so quickly. However, I’m still not completely sure about its accuracy. Every time Codex does something—reads files, searches through code, runs commands—it receives output and adds it to the history. The history grows with every action the agent takes. And every subsequent request you send, even if it’s just three words, is sent to the server together with the entire accumulated history. So if there were three large file searches during a session, that’s already around 600k tokens sitting in the tail. You type “continue” — you send 600k. You type “okay” — another 600k. And so on, until you close the thread. In other words, it seems like the issue might be with how many tokens are being sent with each request. That’s what’s causing such high token usage and, consequently, hitting the limits so quickly. However, this is just a hypothesis, because I’ve noticed some anomalous behavior. Even in a newly created thread, each new request sends an unusually large number of tokens that shouldn’t be there. And most interestingly, the number of tokens being sent keeps increasing with every request. OpenAi, fix pleeeese!

- 2026-04-06T05:57 windysoliloquy https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/23 ::  I am 4% from the limit on one Pro account, and just purchased a second one. I wanted to demonstrate that bridging two separate instances of codex into the same work environment can be handled safely, although it does require a second paid account and some thoughtful foresight. As I don’t know that I’m the best candidate for exploration into this issue, I at least wanted to demonstrate that folks don’t have to go run to Claude to continue their workflow… If folks need an immediate fix to get their workflows going in a codex-only environment, then this is how you do it safely. (Do not forget to back up your entire workspace) That context summary is : Right Here This session established a practical way to hand a live Codex workspace from one ChatGPT account to another without treating the fresh login like a blank slate . The core idea was simple: do not rely on memory crossing accounts. Put the important operating knowledge inside the workspace itself , in plain files that Codex can read on the next login. What we built was a two-file relay system. The first file is the authoritative handoff : CODEX_SECOND_INSTANCE_HANDOFF.md . That became the behavioral contract between Codex instances. It tells a new instance how to start safely, what not to touch, how to handle licensing/signing/release risk, how to read the repo state, how to log its turn, and how to hand off cleanly to the next instance. Over the course of this session, that file was hardened so it did not just protect release and licensing operations, but also Prompt Forge’s product semantics, lane-classification rules, prompt-assembly boundaries, and anti-drift guardrails. It explicitly treats PromptBuilderService as the owner of assembly order, SliderLanguageCatalog as the owner of phrase authority, lane metadata as stable shape only, and policy hooks as exception-only surfaces. It also became the shared source of truth for Codex-to-Codex turn logging. The second file is the workspace companion : CODEX_WORKSPACE_CONTEXT.md . That file does a different job. Instead of telling Codex how to behave, it tells Codex what kind of terrain it is standing in: where the real compiled surfaces are, which files are duplicate shadows, how startup is wired, where machine-local state lives, how shared-lane wiring actually works, where the fragile seams are, and what parts of the app have unusual architectural behavior. It captures the repo as it actually runs, not as an idealized design doc. It also makes clear that it does not override the handoff file; the handoff remains the behavioral authority. That two-file split is what made the bridge work. The handoff file gave the fresh Codex instance: a startup ritual scope discipline anti-churn rules provenance honesty a way to log what it touched a shared baton between accounts The workspace-context file gave the fresh Codex instance: the real runtime composition the project/namespace quirks the duplicate-file trap the lane-install footguns the machine-local workflow surfaces the known validation and release seams Together, those files let a new Codex login enter the workspace with local awareness instead of amnesia . We then proved the bridge was not just theoretical. A second Codex instance used the handoff and companion files to explore the workspace safely, without editing first, and reported back on the actual machine environment. That survey confirmed the installed SDKs, the presence of GitHub CLI and Inno Setup, the existence of real local Prompt Forge preset/demo/license state, and the fact that ChatGPT reasoning was part of the larger working system rather than something fully stored on disk. That was important because it let the workspace notes evolve from “repo notes” into “execution environment notes.” Once the new instance was oriented, the session pushed further and hardened the environment around .NET 8 . A root global.json was added to pin SDK 8.0.419 , and the Codex notes were updated so future runs would treat unexpected .NET 10 selection as drift rather than modernization. That gave both accounts a shared, deterministic SDK baseline. After that, the second instance discovered a build-verification blocker. Instead of guessing, it worked the problem carefully: first separating SDK drift from NuGet access then separating NuGet access from app file locks then separating app file locks from a remaining Diagnostics-project graph issue A Codex-only restore/build lane was created using a local .codex-temp\NuGet.Config plus absolute repo-local DOTNET_* , APPDATA , LOCALAPPDATA , and NUGET_* overrides. That solved the roaming NuGet.Config access problem without changing repo-wide behavior. Once the app itself was closed, Core, LicenseTool, and App all built successfully under that lane. From there, the fresh instance kept using the relay system to investigate the remaining bug safely. It traced the failure into the Diagnostics/App/Core project graph, showed that the problem was not .NET 10 drift, not ordinary NuGet failure, and not a generic workload problem, and narrowed it to a restore/build graph issue involving how Diagnostics expands or reintroduces Core through project references in the full build path. That investigative thread is preserved above in the session log for anyone who wants to study it in detail. So, in practical terms, here is how the cross-account bridge worked: Put the rules in the workspace, not in memory. The new account could not inherit hidden context reliably, so the operating contract was written into CODEX_SECOND_INSTANCE_HANDOFF.md . Put the terrain map in the workspace too. The new account needed to know the repo’s actual runtime shape, duplicate files, validation surfaces, and machine-local seams, so that went into CODEX_WORKSPACE_CONTEXT.md . Make the handoff file the source of truth. Codex-to-Codex logging, status, and baton passing all stayed in the handoff file, so both accounts shared one canonical thread of local execution truth. Let the fresh Codex instance explore read-only first. That avoided blind editing and let it report on SDKs, tools, local state, and constraints before doing any work. Use the notes to constrain behavior, not just describe it. The relay files did not merely document the repo; they actively prevented the new instance from freelancing on lane classification, semantics, labels, release flow, licensing, or architecture. Record every significant turn back into the baton file. That way, the next login did not have to infer what had happened from changed files alone. That is the real lesson of this session: A fresh Codex login can be made safe and effective if the workspace itself contains a disciplined relay layer . Not just notes, but: a behavioral contract a terrain map a logging source of truth a known environment baseline and enough explicit architectural boundaries to prevent “helpful” drift In short, we did not truly “sync accounts.” We synced the workspace so a new account could enter it and behave like a responsible continuation of the old one. The full session can be found here: ChatGPT ChatGPT - Second Pro Account Setup Shared via ChatGPT This uses ChatGPT as a middleman between the codex instances, along with a very specific set of context left in a set of shared relay files between the two codex instances. While this isn’t an immediate fix, it is the best temporary band-aid I could come up with on the fly. (I’m probably more brand loyal than a lot of people) I did find it a little strange that after setting up a new account and doing just this single session, my remaining rate is shown here, using only Gpt5.4 (medium) image_2026-04-06_005358643 214×277 5.12 KB 2 hours is roughly 10 % of my weekly allotment on a pro account. If that math helps at all? My project space is extremely small, as you can see from the Pompt Forge App I’m trying, so hard to deliver to the community as fast as possible ^.^ Anyways, it is entirely possible to extend your workflow into a codex-only space while the current situation is being worked out.

- 2026-04-06T14:11 Mat_Tr https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/36 ::  I want to add a more precise datapoint to this thread. After checking my local Codex logs, the issue does not look like “my request was too large”. The real issue looks like quota/rate-limit accounting being far too expensive for the actual token volume. In my case, a single request showed: total_tokens = 33109 input_tokens = 32924 cached_input_tokens = 30208 output_tokens = 185 And that corresponded to about 14% of the 5-hour quota window. That is the core problem. A request of roughly 33k tokens is not inherently abnormal for Codex. In real coding workflows, large context is expected: system instructions, project context, thread state, tool outputs, and relevant code all add up. That alone is not evidence of a bug. What looks wrong is the pricing/accounting of that request against the quota. If about 33k tokens consumes about 14% of the 5-hour window, then the effective 5-hour capacity is only around 236k tokens. That seems implausibly low for practical Codex usage, and much lower than what many users were previously able to use in comparable workflows. So the issue appears to be this: users are being charged too much quota for a normal amount of tokens. That is why the experience feels broken. The token volume itself is not the main anomaly. The anomaly is how aggressively that token volume is converted into quota consumption. I previously suspected the main problem might be overly inflated input size. After digging into the logs more carefully, I no longer think that is the important part. The more serious problem is that the quota accounting appears disproportionate to the actual token count.

- 2026-04-06T16:56 Mat_Tr https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/42 ::  I have a hypothesis, and in order to confirm or disprove it, I need feedback from people who have also encountered this current issue. As I mentioned earlier in this thread in another message, I noticed that 30,000 tokens cost me 14% of the 5-hour limit. That seems unreasonable, and in my opinion, it should not work that way — I do not think anyone would disagree with that. At the same time, I noticed one detail: many of the people who are experiencing this same problem and posting in this thread mention that they use multiple accounts to work with Codex. That is what led me to this hypothesis. Before this issue appeared — just the day before yesterday — I was using my main account, and the limit was being consumed normally: I was able to work the entire day without hitting the 5-hour cap. But after I ran into the weekly limit, I decided to switch to another account that I had specifically bought as a backup in case the limits on my main account ran out. And immediately after switching to that second account, I noticed that the 5-hour limit started being consumed unreasonably fast. So here is my hypothesis: perhaps a new system has been introduced that detects when someone is using multiple accounts for Codex. And if it sees that a person is using more than one account, it does not block them directly or impose an explicit ban, but instead causes their limits to drain unfairly quickly, making it impossible to work normally. I want to emphasize that this is only a hypothesis. That is why I would really like to hear from others who have also run into this issue: do you use multiple accounts to work with Codex? Is there a pattern here — namely that the people affected by this issue are mostly the ones using multiple accounts? If it turns out that most or all of the people experiencing this problem are indeed using multiple accounts, then perhaps that is exactly where the root cause lies.

The hypothesis was as follows: the anomalous rate limit consumption that some users in this thread are experiencing is most likely related to attempts to bypass usage limits. Previously OpenAI didn’t track this, and the scheme worked fine — you could pay for several Business accounts and switch between them as limits were exhausted. But it appears that in recent days OpenAI has started cracking down: this kind of activity is now being tracked, and affected accounts receive a hidden restriction that makes each request abnormally expensive. To test the hypothesis, I created a clean account, paid for a subscription, and started working on the same projects and tasks — the limits are being consumed correctly. This confirms that my previous accounts had a hidden restriction applied, causing a single request to consume 10–50% of the five-hour limit. This is not 100% confirmation — we haven’t received an official response from OpenAI yet. But it solved my problem and allowed me to continue working normally with Codex. - 2026-04-08T20:51 soakes https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/147 ::  I think OpenAI is in danger of repeating a mistake we have just seen another major developer platform make. In December 2025, GitHub announced a new per-minute platform charge for self-hosted Actions runners on private repositories. The backlash was immediate, and GitHub ended up postponing the change. The issue was not just the price itself. It was the loss of trust that came with it. Once developers start to feel that normal, legitimate usage is being penalised or made unpredictable, they begin looking for alternatives. We have already seen at least some prominent projects move away from GitHub to platforms such as Codeberg. That is why so many people in this thread are raising concerns about Business Codex usage. If routine coding workflows are consuming credit at a rate that makes the product impractical, then this is not a minor pricing complaint. It becomes a product trust problem. The danger for OpenAI is not necessarily an immediate mass exit. The damage usually shows up more slowly than that. Companies often have switching costs, internal dependencies, and data locked into existing workflows, so they do not leave overnight. What happens first is that confidence drops. Teams reduce usage. New adopters hesitate. Existing users start testing competitors and building fallback options. That is the pattern people here are trying to warn about. Trust is relatively easy to build at the start, but extremely hard to win back once users feel they are being priced out of ordinary use. If Business Codex accounts continue burning through credit far faster than users reasonably expect for day-to-day work, OpenAI risks pushing away exactly the people who validate the tool, recommend it internally, and help drive broader adoption. People are not posting here because they want the product to fail. Quite the opposite. Many of us are raising this now because we do not want OpenAI to repeat the same pattern: introduce a model that looks acceptable on paper, underestimate the real-world impact, and only react after the trust damage is already done. OpenAI still has time to correct this, but the window closes quickly once users stop believing that normal usage is sustainable.

- 2026-04-09T08:13 Mat_Tr https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/160 ::  I’m genuinely surprised by the people commenting in this thread without having a Business account and without properly understanding the issue, yet claiming that the problems reported by Business users are “normal.” The fact is, they not only don’t use a Business account, but also have only a superficial understanding of what’s actually being discussed here. Everyone knows — including Business users themselves — that since April 2, the Business plan has become roughly 60% worse than Plus. It’s also common knowledge that the “double limits” promotion has ended. But that’s not what people are complaining about. If the Business plan had simply become 60% worse, as stated, that would be unpleasant but acceptable. Even if it were 100% worse, I could live with that. That’s not the issue. Users are reporting that limits are being consumed abnormally fast. We’re not talking about a 60% difference — we’re talking 500% or more. At that point, the tool becomes unusable. Meanwhile, outside observers seem to assume they know better, concluding that users simply don’t understand how to manage their limits. A simple example: I mentioned that a request containing just the word “hello” could consume up to 5% of a 5-hour limit. The response I got was that I must be using the system incorrectly — instead of considering the obvious possibility that something is wrong. For clarity: this request was made in an empty project, in a new thread, with no prior context and no agent instructions. Clearly, it should not consume that much. Because of this issue, I switched back to my Plus account. Yes, limits there are also being consumed faster than during the promotion, but still within reasonable bounds: over 5 hours of active work, I use about 50%. On the Business account, however, the same workload drains the entire limit in about 10 minutes. That’s not a 60% difference — that’s a critical degradation. As for the cause, it likely relates to the new pricing system introduced on April 2. Usage is now calculated based on tokens rather than message count. For now, this applies only to Business accounts, but it’s expected to roll out to Plus, Pro, and Enterprise plans in the coming weeks. If this system isn’t refined, these same issues will affect everyone. Bugs in newly released systems are normal. That’s exactly why we’re raising this issue — so developers are aware and can address it as quickly as possible, or at least clarify whether this is now the intended pricing model. So I ask those who haven’t encountered this issue not to argue or derail the discussion. It only makes it harder for the team — who have already received these reports via the moderator — to properly review and respond to user feedback.

- 2026-04-10T06:51 merefield https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/168 ::  For those that weren’t clear, please don’t miss the useful explanation of the Business plan here: ChatGPT seats / workspace / codex Codex Hi @Q_Mah, I hear you, the seat vs usage model (and how it maps to tiers/credits) can be confusing, especially when trying to adjust things like seat count. - 2026-04-10T13:14 Mat_Tr https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/177 ::  I think I can finally put an end to this topic, because after a week of testing I now understand how this system works in practice. The main problem is that many people are still trying to evaluate the new pricing model the old way. They see that a request consumed a large percentage of the 5-hour limit and immediately assume that something is broken. But the point is that under the new system, the most useful way to understand Codex usage is through reasoning time . In other words: the longer the agent spends reasoning, the more of your 5-hour limit it consumes. So the practical question is no longer “how many messages do I get?” The practical question now is: how many minutes of reasoning are included in my plan, and how much does each minute cost as a percentage of the 5-hour limit? That is the key to understanding everything. 1. The practical formula If you want a simple and transparent way to estimate usage, you can think about it like this: Cost of a request = reasoning time × percentage cost per minute This is the most useful practical formula for real usage. 2. GPT-5.4 on Plus Based on testing, on the latest GPT-5.4 model, the Plus plan gives you about: 1 hour of reasoning per 5-hour limit window That means: • 40 minutes = 100% of the 5-hour limit • 20 minutes = 50% • 1 minute = about 2.5% So on Plus with GPT-5.4: 1 minute of reasoning costs about 2.5% of the 5-hour limit. 3. GPT-5.3 on Plus GPT-5.3 is about 1.5x more efficient than GPT-5.4 in practice. That means that on Plus, instead of 60 minutes, you get about: 60 minutes of reasoning per 5-hour limit window So: • 60 minutes = 100% • 30 minutes = 50% • 1 minute = about 1.66% So on Plus with GPT-5.3: 1 minute of reasoning costs about 1.66% of the 5-hour limit. 4. Business plan Now to the part that caused the most confusion. After revisiting my earlier calculations, I think I now understand much better what went wrong in how many of us initially interpreted the Business plan. The main issue is this: OpenAI appears to have described the difference between Business and Plus in a way that sounded much softer than the real user experience. Many users understood the claimed “around 60% difference” to mean something like this: Business should drain the limit about 60% faster than Plus. That is the interpretation most people would naturally make, because users do not think in terms of abstract allowance math. They think in terms of what they actually feel during work: how fast the percentage disappears. But based on testing, that does not seem to be what OpenAI meant. What they most likely meant was something different: Business includes less total reasoning allowance than Plus. That is a very different kind of statement. To explain it simply, imagine Plus gives you 100 units of total allowance. If Business gives 60% less , that does not mean the limit drains 60% faster. It means Business gives you only 40 units instead of 100 . So the claim is about the size of the total budget , not the speed at which that budget burns . And that distinction matters a lot. Because once the total budget becomes much smaller, the exact same task starts consuming a much larger percentage of that budget. That is why users may hear “60% less” and expect a moderate downgrade, while in practice the plan feels dramatically worse. Now here is the practical version using my current estimates. If Plus gives about: 40 minutes of GPT-5.4 reasoning per 5-hour window and Business gives only about: 12.5 minutes then the problem becomes much easier to see. This means Business is not just “a little smaller.” It means the total available reasoning time is far smaller. Put differently: Plus gives about 40 minutes Business gives about 12.5 minutes so Business gives only a small fraction of the working time that Plus gives In other words, OpenAI’s framing appears to have been about the remaining total allowance , while users were thinking about how fast the limit disappears during real work . Those are not the same thing. And the gap becomes even more obvious when you look at the practical drain rate. On Plus + GPT-5.4: 1 minute ≈ 2.5% On Business + GPT-5.4: 1 minute ≈ 8% So in real use, Business does not just feel “around 60% worse.” It feels dramatically more restrictive, because the limit drains: 8 / 2.5 = 3.2× faster That means the real-world drain is about: 220% faster per minute than Plus And that is the part many users were never clearly told. There is also another important point here. The issue is not only that the difference appears to have been described using a softer metric. The issue is that even within that softer framing, the real reduction now looks worse than “around 60%.” If my current estimates are correct, then going from 40 minutes on Plus to 12.5 minutes on Business means the actual drop is closer to: about 68.75% less included reasoning time than Plus So even the softer framing may have understated the real reduction. That is why so many Business users felt that something was broken. It may not have been a bug at all. It may simply have been a much more severe regression in practical usability than the wording suggested. This also helps explain why Business users kept reporting that the plan had become nearly unusable, while those complaints were easy to dismiss if someone only looked at the softer allowance framing instead of the real per-minute drain. So if my current estimates are correct, then on Business + GPT-5.4 the practical model is: 12.5 minutes = 100% 1 minute = about 8% That means: 2 minutes = about 16% 4 minutes = about 32% And once you look at it this way, the reports from Business users suddenly stop looking irrational. They start looking completely predictable. That is also why I think OpenAI needs to answer this more directly. If the difference between Business and Plus was communicated in terms of included allowance, why was it not made equally clear that, in real usage, this would translate into a dramatically faster per-minute drain? Because from a mathematical point of view, that framing may be defensible. But from a user point of view, it was not transparent enough — and it made the regression feel far less serious on paper than it turned out to be in practice. 5. Business plan on GPT-5.3 If you switch to GPT-5.3, which appears to be more efficient, then Business gives roughly: about 18.75 minutes of reasoning per 5-hour limit window That means: 18.75 minutes = 100% 1 minute = about 5.33% So on Business + GPT-5.3 , a rough practical estimate is: 1 minute of reasoning costs about 5.33% of the 5-hour limit. This also explains why the same task can feel noticeably cheaper on GPT-5.3 than on GPT-5.4, even on the same Business plan. For example, a 4-minute run would roughly cost: 4 × 5.33% = about 21.3% That is still expensive, but clearly better than GPT-5.4 on Business. 6. Very important: reasoning level does not directly change the price This part is also important. The reasoning level itself — low, medium, high, or very high — does not directly change the price . A 4-minute run costs the same percentage whether the reasoning level was low or very high. What changes is that higher reasoning levels usually make the model think longer. So the difference is not that “high reasoning is priced higher.” The difference is that: higher reasoning usually leads to longer reasoning time, and longer reasoning time costs more. That is why people often think the reasoning level itself is more expensive, when in reality it is the extra time that increases the cost. 7. How to use this in practice This is the most important takeaway for anyone trying to manage limits more intelligently. If you want transparency, then stop thinking in terms of message count and start thinking in terms of included reasoning minutes . That gives you a much clearer way to plan your usage. For example: • if you need the strongest model, use GPT-5.4, but expect it to burn limits faster; • if you want more total working time, GPT-5.3 is more economical; • if a task is likely to take several minutes of agent reasoning, you can now estimate the cost in advance; • if you are on Business, you should expect the limit to disappear much faster than on Plus. So instead of guessing, you can now roughly calculate the cost before running a task. That is the whole point of this post. 8. Final conclusion The issue is not that people are crazy for thinking something was wrong. The issue is that the new system was not transparent enough, so users had no clear mental model for how to interpret the new limit behavior. Now we do. The most practical way to understand the current system is this: • your real cost is tied to reasoning time, • each model has a different cost per minute, • Business includes much less than Plus, • and once you understand the approximate percentage-per-minute formula, the system becomes much more predictable. 9. Where Pro 5X fits into this There is also an important clarification to make about the new Pro 5X plan, because it helps explain the current Codex limit system even more clearly. What matters here is not just that Pro 5X is a paid upgrade. What matters is that, in practice, it gives a much larger amount of available reasoning time relative to Plus — and under the current promotion, that increase becomes large enough that limits can almost disappear from normal workflows. Using Plus as the baseline: on GPT-5.4, a 5-hour limit window appears to include roughly 40 minutes of reasoning time . That means: 20 minutes = about 50% of the limit 40 minutes = about 100% of the limit Now, the new Pro 5X subscription costs $100 and is designed around a 5× Codex allowance relative to Plus . But under the temporary promotion running until May 31, 2026 , it effectively provides 10× the Plus allowance instead of 5× . So in practical GPT-5.4 terms, that means: Plus ≈ 40 minutes of reasoning per 5-hour window Pro 5X during the promotion ≈ 400 minutes of reasoning per 5-hour window That is roughly: 6.7 hours of reasoning time inside a single 5-hour limit window From a practical point of view, that is enormous. For many real work scenarios, this can already be treated as effectively removing the limit problem altogether — especially for users working in Fast mode. This is also why Pro 5X is such an interesting option: compared to the older and more expensive Pro 20× for $200 , the new $100 Pro 5X plan may actually feel more efficient or more attractive in practice, because under the current promotion it behaves like a 10× plan , while costing much less. In other words, for $100 , users can temporarily get a level of Codex access that is large enough to stop thinking about limits in everyday work and simply use the system at full speed. That is why Pro 5X should not be viewed as just “another subscription tier.” Under the current promo, it is much closer to: a practical way to get rid of limit anxiety and work at maximum pace without constantly watching your percentage. So if Plus is the baseline for understanding reasoning cost, and Business is the plan where the drain feels noticeably aggressive, then Pro 5X — especially during the 10× promotion — is the tier where limits begin to feel almost irrelevant for serious daily use.

- 2026-04-10T13:42 windysoliloquy https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/179 ::  Mat_Tr: the longer the agent spends reasoning, the more of your 5-hour limit it consumes. This has been mentioned in the thread in other ways… (Also we tend to like long walls of text tucked into these tidy boxes -) My best tip for codex usage You don’t let your bot think… use gpt 5.4 to do your project reasoning, and have it write the prompts for codex… You’ll find that it over writes the prompts often. You’ll find that you’ll want a specific container for your project files, specifically around architecture, and the names your project invokes as calls, files, and any special wiring you’ve produce in the project. You’ll find that once it gets settled that it will still overwrite constraints and be repetitive. That can be quickly fixed with some text select and backspace key usage after you get a feel for what your codex session already has locked in as far as handling that sort of work on your project. I think one of the most important constraints is ‘don’t invent new ways to accomplish (your goal). I’ve defaulted to a flow where I give codex a prompt written by 5.4 that starts off with my typing at the top - ”This prompt is submitted for your examination only, how well does this prompt meet the narrow scope of my goal,do not execute”: [pasted 5.4 prompt] (The : is important in that) It will then return a report about the strengths and weaknesses of your prompt, where it might get confused in theory, and you adjust from there. If it’s good I tell it to handle the last prompt as it’s marching orders rather than paste the same wall of text twice. When Codex says a long report of what that 5.4 prompt sucks, which can happen sometimes, you copy that response and take it back to 5.4 and work it out. you do that a few times and you’ll see 5.4 and codex start to calibrate with each other and you become a mediator rather than tossing ambiguous prompts at codex that waste a lot of tokens. Now I don’t know if what I just said is something aligned with or completely outside of what OpenAi has suggested to do with codex, it just works for me naturally after a couple of weeks of tooling with it. Most important part I can see from your post is there’s probably not enough constraints in your prompt, and it also implies you might be too ambiguous to begin with. Bring 5.4 into your loop and after a few cycles i think you’ll be happier. It does cause the codex to ‘think’ as it examines your prompts, which costs you… but it’s worth it to get that session locked in to your goal and workflow at the start of it rather than spend a bunch of tokens on reasoning.

What they most likely meant was something different: Business includes less total reasoning allowance than Plus. That is a very different kind of statement. To explain it simply, imagine Plus gives you 100 units of total allowance. If Business gives 60% less , that does not mean the limit drains 60% faster. It means Business gives you only 40 units instead of 100 . So the claim is about the size of the total budget , not the speed at which that budget burns . And that distinction matters a lot. Because once the total budget becomes much smaller, the exact same task starts consuming a much larger percentage of that budget. That is why users may hear “60% less” and expect a moderate downgrade, while in practice the plan feels dramatically worse. Now here is the practical version using my current estimates. If Plus gives about: 40 minutes of GPT-5.4 reasoning per 5-hour window and Business gives only about: 12.5 minutes then the problem becomes much easier to see. This means Business is not just “a little smaller.” It means the total available reasoning time is far smaller. Put differently: Plus gives about 40 minutes Business gives about 12.5 minutes so Business gives only a small fraction of the working time that Plus gives In other words, OpenAI’s framing appears to have been about the remaining total allowance , while users were thinking about how fast the limit disappears during real work . Those are not the same thing. And the gap becomes even more obvious when you look at the practical drain rate. On Plus + GPT-5.4: 1 minute ≈ 2.5% On Business + GPT-5.4: 1 minute ≈ 8% So in real use, Business does not just feel “around 60% worse.” It feels dramatically more restrictive, because the limit drains: 8 / 2.5 = 3.2× faster That means the real-world drain is about: 220% faster per minute than Plus And that is the part many users were never clearly told. There is also another important point here. The issue is not only that the difference appears to have been described using a softer metric. The issue is that even within that softer framing, the real reduction now looks worse than “around 60%.” If my current estimates are correct, then going from 40 minutes on Plus to 12.5 minutes on Business means the actual drop is closer to: about 68.75% less included reasoning time than Plus So even the softer framing may have understated the real reduction. That is why so many Business users felt that something was broken. It may not have been a bug at all. It may simply have been a much more severe regression in practical usability than the wording suggested. This also helps explain why Business users kept reporting that the plan had become nearly unusable, while those complaints were easy to dismiss if someone only looked at the softer allowance framing instead of the real per-minute drain. So if my current estimates are correct, then on Business + GPT-5.4 the practical model is: 12.5 minutes = 100% 1 minute = about 8% That means: 2 minutes = about 16% 4 minutes = about 32% And once you look at it this way, the reports from Business users suddenly stop looking irrational. They start looking completely predictable. That is also why I think OpenAI needs to answer this more directly. If the difference between Business and Plus was communicated in terms of included allowance, why was it not made equally clear that, in real usage, this would translate into a dramatically faster per-minute drain? Because from a mathematical point of view, that framing may be defensible. But from a user point of view, it was not transparent enough — and it made the regression feel far less serious on paper than it turned out to be in practice. 5. Business plan on GPT-5.3 If you switch to GPT-5.3, which appears to be more efficient, then Business gives roughly: about 18.75 minutes of reasoning per 5-hour limit window That means: 18.75 minutes = 100% 1 minute = about 5.33% So on Business + GPT-5.3 , a rough practical estimate is: 1 minute of reasoning costs about 5.33% of the 5-hour limit. This also explains why the same task can feel noticeably cheaper on GPT-5.3 than on GPT-5.4, even on the same Business plan. For example, a 4-minute run would roughly cost: 4 × 5.33% = about 21.3% That is still expensive, but clearly better than GPT-5.4 on Business.

- 2026-04-11T15:15 Mat_Tr https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/195 ::  I don’t think the answer is actually that complicated, although this is just my personal view. From a business perspective, I think the key difference is how people actually consume the included limits in ChatGPT versus Codex. Most ChatGPT users probably consume only a small fraction of the usage that is technically included in their subscription. In other words, the allowance is there, but the average user never comes close to fully using it. To make it simple, imagine that a ChatGPT Plus subscription includes 100% of some available usage budget, but the average ChatGPT user only consumes, say, 10% of that in practice. For the company, that is a very comfortable model, because most users are not pushing the system anywhere near the maximum. Codex, in my view, is used very differently. People who use Codex are much more likely to push it hard and burn through a large share of the included allowance. So instead of an average user consuming a small fraction, you may be looking at something more like 50% on average, or even close to full exhaustion for heavy users. And that starts to hit OpenAI’s economics much harder. That is why I do not think ChatGPT and Codex can be compared purely on the basis of “same infrastructure, same tokens.” From a business standpoint, what matters is not only the underlying compute, but also the average intensity of usage across the user base. We recently saw something very similar with Anthropic. Once people started using their subscription through OpenClaw and effectively burning the full included limits through agent-style workflows, it began to hurt their business economics. And as you probably know, Anthropic eventually prohibited using subscriptions that way: if you connect a subscription through OpenClaw instead of using the API, the account can be banned. So my view is this: the issue is probably not that ChatGPT is inherently cheap for OpenAI while Codex is inherently expensive. The issue is that Codex users, on average, consume their included limits far more aggressively than ordinary ChatGPT users do. And that is probably what forced OpenAI to change the economics of Codex inside these subscription tiers.

- 2026-04-13T20:30 Engineer1 https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/213 ::  Caveman seems genuinely useful for workflows that are mostly machine-facing or that produce fixed-form artifacts for humans. In those cases, the degraded prose is mostly confined to exception paths, debugging, or terse status output. In that mode, compressed prose matters much less because the bar is mostly external: does the thing appear to work. That said, I do not think this mode creates much lasting economic value. Real value usually comes from systems that remain understandable, extensible, operable, and trustworthy over time. My objection is about deep collaborative work. When I work closely with an agent, I am not just extracting conclusions from it — I am spending hours inside its language. Prolonged exposure means I absorb patterns of phrasing, compression, rhythm, and thought. If those patterns are intentionally degraded for token efficiency, they may still bleed into my own sense of expression. I do not want a large fraction of my reading environment to consist of deliberately broken, impoverished English. Also, “it only affects what it outputs, not the code” is a much stronger claim than the evidence supports. Their evals mainly show that the style reduces output tokens. They do not seriously prove that the technical result stays the same, let alone that the code stays the same. So the burden of proof for “same code output” is nowhere near met. And for me this becomes even more problematic because I strongly believe projects should have .md specs in the repo, and the agent should be able to edit them. That is where caveman starts to actively worry me. The style will not stay neatly confined to disposable chat replies — it will bleed into the spec itself. And if it starts editing an existing spec under compression pressure, I worry it will collapse context: flatten nuance, drop still-valid ideas, and gradually erode the reasoning that made the spec useful in the first place.

- 2026-04-16T10:57 OzkanVirex https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/259 ::  TL;DR: Codex limits used to feel generous and workable, even for regular users like me who were not exhausting them. The recent reduction feels extremely severe, not minor. I understand the possible business logic behind pushing some users toward higher-tier plans, but this risks damaging long-term user habits and retention. In the AI market, losing users over preventable limit policy may be a much bigger mistake than it looks in the short term. Codex limits used to reset quite often before their scheduled expiration. Because of that, I was not just happy with the product, but genuinely grateful. Also, except for maybe one time, I was never even a user who fully exhausted my limits. I was not someone using Codex at an extreme level and draining every bit of quota; in most weeks, I still had around 50% to 70% of my weekly limit left before it reset. That is why I felt comfortable recommending Codex on social platforms. I often reassured people that they probably would not run into serious limit issues. Then, suddenly, the limits were tightened very heavily. This was not a small nerf. It was a major one. The difference between the old experience and the current rate of limit consumption is enormous. I sometimes wonder whether the thinking is something like this: if one person upgrades to the $120 plan, that offsets the loss of six other users. That way, server load goes down while revenue stays balanced. I can understand that logic. However, there is something extremely important being overlooked here: user habits and long-term retention. Right now, many of us may not leave immediately simply because of habit. But once the limits make regular usage feel impractical, and once that becomes noticeable enough, people may no longer have much choice but to look elsewhere. And once users move to other platforms, getting them to come back may be much harder than expected. Especially at the dawn of the AI era, losing customers matters a lot. Losing them for a preventable reason like limit policy could become a much bigger mistake in the long run. On paper, losing six users and replacing them with one higher-paying user may look efficient. But if you eventually lose that one user too, then you have actually concentrated your risk. In other words, replacing six customers with one means a single future loss now costs six times more. For that reason, I would strongly recommend reconsidering the recent changes to Codex limits. I believe there is room for a more reasonable balance between customer satisfaction and resource management.

- 2026-04-17T15:10 windysoliloquy https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/269 ::  Realizing Mental Model isn’t the right technical term: I ask codex this: Well you use the term sometimes and I’m wondering if asking you to dump your mental model of my repo into a doc, if it would help 5.4 mini not be so helplessly lost on a project It responds with this: Yes, that can help a lot. What I call a “mental model” is usually closest to: working model conceptual model codebase model For your use case, I’d phrase it as: working model of the repo or codebase operating model And yes, dumping that into a doc can materially help a smaller model like gpt-5.4-mini , especially on a project like Prompt Forge where: architecture rules matter a lot wording authority lives in specific seams there are many “don’t touch this, work there instead” boundaries recent local conventions aren’t obvious from code alone What helps mini most is not a giant repo summary, but a focused operating doc: what the major seams are which files own what what patterns are normal what patterns are forbidden where recent work has concentrated what “good changes” look like in this repo So the useful artifact is less “my mental model” in a vague sense, and more: Repo Working Model Codebase Operating Model Prompt Forge Implementation Model If you asked me to create one, I’d make it short and practical, with sections like: architecture ownership lane system rules phrase authority pair-collapse rules protected surfaces common failure modes current active work zones That kind of doc absolutely can make a smaller model less lost. It won’t make mini brilliant, but it can make it much less directionless. So, considering that… there might actually be hope for mini if we take the time to dump the project rules into it via 5.4… Requires testing to verify, always back up your projects before you start testing, if you choose to. ^.^ This Ai response might prove helpful too: Sorry, it’s directed more at my current project than general knowledge- Codex is basically right. The important correction is this: the useful artifact is not a mystical “mental model dump.” It is a practical repo operating document that tells the agent how your codebase is organized, what owns what, what is forbidden, and what counts as a correct change. That lines up with OpenAI’s current guidance: Codex works best with the right task context, a clear structure for the task, and durable repo guidance in AGENTS.md . ( OpenAI Developers ) The term I would use is: Repo Working Model for the longer explanatory document. AGENTS.md for the compact, auto-loaded rules Codex should always see. ( OpenAI Developers ) “Codebase operating model” is a good human label, but it is not the official mechanism Codex is built around. The official mechanism is AGENTS.md , plus optional skills for repeatable workflows. Codex loads project guidance from AGENTS.md , can layer instructions by directory, and can use repo-scoped skills from .agents/skills . ( OpenAI Developers ) So the strongest setup for Prompt Forge is not one doc, but two layers : 1. A short root AGENTS.md This should contain the hard rules that must survive every session: ownership boundaries protected files/surfaces normal change patterns forbidden patterns how to validate a change what “done” means That matches OpenAI’s advice almost exactly: repo layout, important directories, conventions, constraints, and verification criteria belong there, and it should stay short and practical rather than becoming a giant manifesto. ( OpenAI Developers ) 2. A longer Repo Working Model doc This is where you explain Prompt Forge’s deeper structure: LaneRegistry owns lane metadata SliderLanguageCatalog owns phrase authority PromptBuilderService owns orchestration pair-collapse rules standard vs special lane behavior selector remaps checkbox suppression interactions current active work zones common failure modes That longer doc is not mainly for auto-loading. It is for targeted reference when a task is nontrivial. OpenAI’s docs push the same idea indirectly: give Codex the right task context, not a pile of vague instructions, and encode reusable recurring guidance in durable places. ( OpenAI Developers ) On the actual question — would this help 5.4 mini stop getting lost? Yes, very likely. That is an inference, but a grounded one. OpenAI says Codex gets more reliable in larger and higher-stakes codebases when you provide the right context and structure, and says GPT-5.4 is good at following repo-specific patterns and handling multi-file changes. A smaller model will usually benefit even more from well-organized repo guidance because it has less room to recover from ambiguity. ( OpenAI Developers ) What will not help much is a giant free-form repo summary. Codex’s own answer was right on that. OpenAI explicitly says to keep AGENTS.md small and practical, and Codex only loads a limited amount of project instruction content by default—32 KiB combined unless you raise the cap. ( OpenAI Developers ) So the blunt recommendation is: Do not ask Codex to “dump its mental model.” Ask it to produce: A repo operating guide for future coding agents , with sections for: architecture ownership allowed edit surfaces forbidden refactors lane-specific conventions semantic phrasing authority pair-collapse invariants validation/check steps current hot zones / active workstreams examples of good vs bad changes That framing is tighter, more technical, and much more reusable. For your repo specifically, I would name the long document one of these: docs/REPO_WORKING_MODEL.md docs/PROMPT_FORGE_IMPLEMENTATION_MODEL.md docs/CODEBASE_OPERATING_MODEL.md Then keep AGENTS.md as the distilled version Codex always ingests. If you want Codex to auto-recognize a nonstandard filename as an instructions file, its config supports fallback filenames such as TEAM_GUIDE.md , but that is an extra step; AGENTS.md is the path of least resistance. ( OpenAI Developers ) The last piece is underrated: for recurring repo-specific workflows, use skills , not just prose. OpenAI’s current Codex docs position skills as the right home for reusable workflows and domain expertise, and repo skills live under .agents/skills . For Prompt Forge, that could mean a skill for “ordinary lane install,” one for “pair-collapse update,” one for “selector remap pass,” and one for “Prompt Forge architecture audit.” ( OpenAI Developers ) So the answer is: Yes, make the doc. But make it as a repo operating model plus a short AGENTS.md, not as a vague mental-model brain dump. That is the version most likely to materially improve mini. I can draft the exact structure for both files in Prompt Forge terms.

- 2026-05-20T14:52 OpenAI_Support https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/304 ::  Hey all, wanted to add a bit more context on how Codex usage is counted and what’s worth checking when usage feels higher than expected. Codex usage is not based on “number of prompts” or wall-clock time. It depends on the model, task size, repo/context size, local vs cloud execution, and token usage. The current Codex pricing/rate card is here . A few things that can make usage move faster than expected: - Large repos, long threads, big `AGENTS.md` files, MCP/tool context, and repeated compactions can all increase input/cached/output tokens. - Model choice matters. For example, GPT-5.5 is more expensive than GPT-5.4 / GPT-5.4-mini on the Codex rate card. - Fast mode uses more credits: https://developers.openai.com/codex/speed - Cloud tasks, code review, subagents, and automations can keep doing work in the background. Automations are documented here: https://developers.openai.com/codex/app/automations - Memories can also do background work. Codex may update memory files after a thread has been idle long enough causing this impacts your usage. You can review/control this via Codex settings or `/memories`, and memory files live under `~/.codex/memories/`. So if your app looks “idle” but usage still moved, the first things I’d check are: 1. Codex Settings → Usage & billing. 2. Run `/status` in an active CLI session. 3. Check whether any cloud tasks, code reviews, automations, subagents, or background terminal runs are still active. 4. [Most common] Check whether Memories are enabled with `/memories` or in Codex settings. 5. Compare the model/reasoning/Fast mode setting used for the run. If something still looks wrong, you should submit a support request to us at support@openai.com and our team can look into your usage. The most useful support report is: plan type, model, local/cloud, exact timestamp + timezone, before/after usage screenshot, and whether Memories/automations/subagents/cloud tasks were enabled. That gives the team enough info to separate “expected token/background usage” from a real accounting issue. We cannot attain those information publicly so we request you to please use the support path. We appreciate and thank everyone for coming together here and engaging the community.

- 2026-05-22T23:08 windysoliloquy https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/307 ::  Are these requests in threads that have a lot of previous requests? I ask that because I recently had to transplant a project that I rebuilt into it’s own repo. I found out that the size of the repo effects your credit usage as well. One of several key things that helped me reduce credit spend was looking out for many artifacts that are being drug around with the session. If you’re using the desktop app and it’s not full screen, just move your mouse to about the middle of the right edge, and move it up along the edge about 20%… Check to see how many artifacts you’re carrying around. image 294×234 4.07 KB 13 is a pretty big number I’ve got 1 session that drags over 60 docs around as artifacts, it seems like this has an effect on overspending too. Be mindful of what docs your bot needs in order to have the map it can use safely… Ask it to aduit the docs it needs for future work.. When say, an engine is completed and you’re building around it put it inside of the agents.md that it shouldn’t be edited unless absolutely required… I’ve discovered there’s a lot of things that can bloat your consumption, but one of the main things is not letting automation check things you can do with a visual test yourself.

- 2026-08-19T13:19 tiagopatriciosantos https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/394 ::  I wonder if part of the recent sudden increase in Codex usage could be related to larger context usage, particularly in long-running sessions. There are a few interesting facts that seem worth connecting. According to OpenAI’s official GPT-5.6 Sol model documentation, the model supports a 1,050,000-token context window and up to 128K output tokens . The current Codex model metadata for GPT-5.6 Sol shows: context_window: 272000 max_context_window: 872000 So 272K remains the default, while Codex now supports opting into a substantially larger context window. More importantly, OpenAI’s GPT-5.6 Sol documentation states that prompts with more than 272K input tokens are priced at 2x input and 1.5x output for the full request . OpenAI’s Codex usage documentation also explains that larger codebases, long-running tasks, and extended sessions that require Codex to hold more context can use significantly more usage per message. Tibo Sottiaux from the Codex team also recently explained how to enable the larger context window for GPT-5.6 Sol, and in a separate post noted that there had been a recent change to Codex’s context handling, while also emphasizing that the normal Codex context limit was tuned with performance and cost in mind. That makes me wonder whether some of the unexpectedly high Codex quota consumption users are seeing could be related to substantially more context being retained or processed during long-running sessions — particularly for users using the extended context window, or if anything recently changed in how much context Codex retains between turns. I am not claiming this is definitely the cause . The >272K multiplier refers to API pricing, and we do not know exactly how ChatGPT/Codex subscription quota accounting maps to API token pricing. But it would be useful if the Codex team could clarify: Has anything recently changed in the amount of context Codex retains or processes during long-running sessions, and does using the extended context window — particularly beyond ~272K tokens — materially increase the rate at which ChatGPT/Codex subscription usage limits are consumed? If so, that could explain at least part of the sudden usage increase users are reporting.

- 2026-08-22T17:07 ANS1337 https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/406 ::  Hi, everyone. Evidence is increasingly pointing to the fact that the Codex Pro x5 (and possibly x20) weekly allowance has dropped by ~30–33% in recent weeks — measured from local rollout logs, not vibes. I encourage you to check out the analysis below. I initially thought I was imagining this. However, after finding more than a few similar opinions on this topic (including within this Community): For previous months, I could use Codex noticeably more on a Prolite (x5) plan account, and the weekly allowance behaved reasonably and consistently. Recently, however, (roughly since the beginning of August), my weekly limit started disappearing dramatically faster - even though I deliberately moved most of the workload to GPT-5.6 Terra, which is substantially cheaper than Sol - as much as 60% less per token! So instead of guessing, I audited my local Codex rollout history, and the result is concerning… I reconstructed several historical Codex weekly windows from local rollout-*.jsonl files and compared the amount of locally recorded model workload required for the backend to report 100% weekly usage. The comparison uses the exact same normalization function and the same published Codex rate card for every historical window. I am NOT comparing raw token counts between models. I normalize: uncached input cached input output using the same model-specific rates for every period. I also use cumulative ‘total_token_usage’ deltas instead of summing ‘last_token_usage’, so duplicated ‘token_count’ snapshots are not counted repeatedly. Historical weekly windows may appear under “secondary”, while newer weekly windows appear under “primary”; both are identified by the 10080-minute weekly window. For 100% windows, accounting stops at the first observed used_percent = 100. Here are the clean results: Period Account Backend usage Normalized workload at limit Jun 11–16 Pro x5 account A 100% 14,381 Jun 30 – Jul 5 Pro x5 account B 99% ~15,024 implied Jul 12–14 Pro x5 account B 100% 14,202 Aug 13–17 Pro x5 account B 100% 13,514 Aug 20–22 Pro x5 account B 100% 9,613 All of these sessions report the same Codex plan type: Prolite the older clean Pro x5 windows cluster around roughly 14–15k normalized credit-equivalent workload. The current weekly window reached 100% at only: 9,613 (!). Compared with the historical clean median of approximately: 14,381, that is about a 33% reduction in effective observed weekly capacity. Even compared only with the immediately previous weekly window on the SAME account: 13,514 → 9,613 that is approximately 28.9% less normalized workload before reaching 100%. The model mix does not explain this. The current weekly workload was: GPT-5.6 Terra: 87.4% of tokens GPT-5.6 Sol: 12.5% GPT-5.6 Luna: 0.08% After normalization: Terra accounted for ~69.8% of the weighted workload Sol accounted for ~30.2% So yes, Sol requests were relatively expensive, but that cost is already included in the comparison. The entire point of the normalization is that an August week with a high Terra volume can be compared against an older Sol-heavy week using the same cost function. For example: Jul 12–14 was essentially 100% GPT-5.6 Sol and reached 100% around 14,202 normalized units. Aug 20–22 was overwhelmingly GPT-5.6 Terra and reached 100% around 9,613 normalized units. This is not simply “Terra used more tokens”. The current week actually processed approximately 1.19 billion aggregate token traffic before reaching 100%, because most input was cached and Terra is cheaper. So the question is: Why did the backend weekly percentage reach 100% after dramatically less normalized workload? I am NOT claiming that this proves OpenAI secretly reduced Pro x5 limits by exactly 33%, although it is tempting… There are several possible explanations: the underlying weekly allowance was reduced model-specific weekly metering multipliers changed the backend is charging additional activity that is not represented in local ‘total_token_usage’ there is a quota/accounting bug some other Codex metering rule changed However, something measurable changed. The strongest evidence is that multiple earlier weekly windows, across two separate Pro x5 accounts, cluster around the same ~14–15k normalized threshold, while the newest window stops at ~9.6k . This is not based on how the progress bar “felt”. It is reconstructed from thousands of local Codex ‘token_count’ records and backend-reported weekly ‘used_percent’ My current weekly window: started: Aug 20, ~08:30 CEST reached 100%: Aug 22, ~17:12 CEST normalized workload before 100%: ~9,613 I would really like OpenAI to clarify: Did the effective Codex weekly allowance for Pro x5 / prolite change in August? If not, what changed in the metering formula that causes ‘used_percent’ to reach 100% after ~30–33% less normalized workload than historical windows? If other Pro users have local Codex rollout history (and you definitely do), especially from June/July and August, I strongly encourage you to compare your own 10080-minute weekly windows. This should be reproducible. If you don’t know how to do this, please let me know in this thread. I’ll explain how it’s done. Best regards.

- 2026-08-25T20:36 Copel https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/432 ::  That comparison is interesting, but I don’t think it proves what you’re suggesting. “API-equivalent spend” is not the same thing as OpenAI’s marginal cost of providing the service. API pricing includes margin, infrastructure overhead, capacity management, enterprise economics, and a pricing model designed for a very different usage pattern. So saying that a $200 subscription gives you thousands of dollars of “API-equivalent value” does not mean OpenAI is actually losing thousands of dollars on that subscriber. More importantly, heavy subsidisation — even if we assume that is exactly what is happening — doesn’t answer the criticism about changing limits. Users are not complaining because they believe compute should be free forever. They are complaining because they make workflow and purchasing decisions based on the product that is being sold to them today. If effective capacity is reduced while the headline price remains unchanged, that is a meaningful degradation of the product from the customer’s perspective. The fact that the estimated value has fallen by roughly 33% in a matter of weeks actually strengthens that concern rather than dismissing it. It shows how quickly the practical value of the subscription can change independently of its advertised monthly price. There is also a difference between saying, “This deal is currently very generous,” and saying, “Therefore customers should stop complaining about reductions.” Both things can be true: OpenAI can be offering excellent value relative to API pricing, while users can reasonably object to unpredictable quotas or declining capacity. And “you’ll have to pay more in the future anyway” is not much of a counterargument. Competition exists precisely because customers can compare price, reliability, limits, model quality and workflow. If another coding agent offers a more predictable or economical setup for someone’s particular workload, evaluating it is rational, not ungrateful. So yes, it may be a great deal today. But customers are buying a service, not receiving a gift. Calling it a “gift horse” confuses a commercial transaction with charity. The relevant question is not how generous the provider feels the subsidy is; it is whether the product continues to deliver enough predictable value for the price the customer pays.

- 2026-08-25T21:34 MartynForge https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/434 ::  I think this is a remarkably short-sighted way to look at a technology market. First, you are implicitly assuming that OpenAI’s current cost structure and quota model are somehow representative of the inevitable future of AI coding. They aren’t. Jules, for example, already uses a very different model: autonomous cloud tasks are accounted for as tasks rather than making the user obsess over every agent turn inside a 5-hour bucket. There are also increasingly capable self-hosted and open tooling options — OpenClaw, Hermes and others — where the economics become partly a question of whether investing in your own hardware makes sense. And “AI will simply get more expensive in the future” is a strange prediction for computing. There was a time when 256 MB of RAM was expensive hardware. Today 16 GB is ordinary consumer equipment. Compute gets cheaper, hardware improves, inference becomes more efficient, models get distilled, cached and quantized, and competitors continuously attack each other’s margins. Demand may grow faster than those efficiencies, sure. But presenting today’s API-equivalent retail price as evidence that users should simply expect to pay more forever ignores essentially the entire history of the technology industry. There is another problem with the “$2,000 of API value for $200” argument. Retail API pricing is not OpenAI’s marginal compute cost. Your screenshot is extrapolating an estimated retail API-equivalent value from 4% usage of one account. Interesting? Yes. Evidence that OpenAI is literally spending $2,000 to provide a $200 subscription? No. More importantly, none of this actually answers my criticism. I am not asking for infinite compute for $20. I had 89% of my weekly allocation still available and was being prevented from using it during a productive session because a second artificial 5-hour bucket was nearly exhausted. That is a product-design complaint, not a demand for unlimited free GPUs. And “stop grumbling, you’re getting a great deal” is perhaps the weakest part of the argument. This is not a gift horse. It is a commercial service. Customers do not owe corporations gratitude for choosing a pricing model that the corporation itself decided to sell. Technology markets improve precisely because customers complain, compare alternatives, leave products that stop serving them, and force vendors to compete. The alternative mindset — “be grateful for whatever the company gives you and stop complaining” — is how products stagnate. I don’t work for OpenAI. I don’t work for Google. I don’t work for Anthropic. They compete for my money and my workload. If OpenAI offers the best tool under acceptable conditions, I use OpenAI. If Jules handles my workflow better, I use Jules. If a self-hosted agent plus hardware becomes economically superior, I’ll use that. That is not “looking a gift horse in the mouth.” That is how a functioning market is supposed to work. And if my response to a restrictive quota system is to move the workload to a competitor, then congratulations: I did exactly what you suggested. I optimised my workflow. 
- 2026-08-25T22:24 sebastien1a https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/435 ::  Hello (original French message below), I would also like to share my recent experience following the return of the 5-hour limits for Plus users. I have been using Codex for some time now with the Sol model at High or Very High reasoning, and while I understand the arguments that have been given for this limit, I do not think they are sufficient, and I believe other alternatives could achieve the same goals. First of all, the issue for paying users, as many people have already pointed out, is the level of control we have over our own experience and our ability to use our weekly quota as we see fit. We are paying for that quota, so it seems legitimate to be able to decide how we use it. If allowing that would create too much load, there are middle-ground solutions, and I mention some of them among my suggestions below. In my case, with my current Codex project, I hit the 5-hour limit very quickly, and when I check my weekly usage once my 5-hour allowance has reached 0, I have used barely 15% of my weekly quota. As a safeguard, this feels both frustrating and excessive. I think there are several possible middle-ground solutions: Let users manually choose what percentage of their weekly quota they are willing to consume within a 5-hour period, potentially with a maximum such as 40% or 50% of the total weekly allowance. Offer an intermediate paid subscription between Plus and Pro, with higher usage limits and either no 5-hour limit or the ability to fully control how much of the quota can be used within those 5 hours. Introduce a system that either warns users when they are consuming a large amount of quota over a short period and lets them decide what to do, or temporarily blocks further consumption of their remaining quota until the end of the current 5-hour window while still giving them an option in Settings to unlock it and continue their Work/Codex tasks. Provide an option to enable or disable the 5-hour limit, enabled by default for new Plus users so that they are made aware of how much they are consuming, while still allowing them to decide for themselves whether they want to continue. At the very least, for users who reach their 5-hour usage limit, before preventing an ongoing task from continuing, automatically send a message to the agent asking it to properly wrap up what it is currently doing, so that users do not risk losing data or being left with partially completed work that could be lost. I hope these thoughts and suggestions will be useful and will be seen by the OpenAI teams. Please feel free to pass them on. For now, the project is private, but my long-term goal is potentially to turn it into a proper public project once it is mature enough and can be developed in a more professional environment. The idea is not to build another profit-driven communication platform. The fundamental goal behind TrueCord is user privacy, security and giving users more control over their communication. Codex has become an important part of how I develop this project. And the restored 5-hour limit is making that workflow extremely difficult. Today, I asked Codex to implement roughly five relatively small UI changes. That was enough to consume essentially my entire 5-hour allowance. Meanwhile, I still have more than 90% of my weekly allowance remaining. This is the part of the current system that I simply do not understand. If I have more than 90% of my weekly quota available, why am I prevented from using it? I completely understand that OpenAI needs usage limits. Compute is not unlimited, and I am not asking for unlimited access. A weekly quota is perfectly reasonable because I can plan around it. If I decide to use 40% of my weekly allowance during one intensive development session, that should be my decision. The additional 5-hour restriction removes that flexibility. Software development does not happen in evenly distributed five-hour windows. Sometimes I barely use Codex for several days, and then I have a day where I work intensively on a complex part of the project. My prompts are also inherently expensive. TrueCord is not a collection of isolated scripts. It is a fairly complex system with a large existing codebase, architecture, encryption requirements, backend/frontend interactions, deployment infrastructure, and a lot of project context that Codex needs to understand before making changes safely. That means even seemingly small changes can require substantial context and reasoning. There is another issue that I find even more disruptive: continuity. When I hit the limit during an active development workflow, I cannot simply continue where I was working. I have to wait for the usage window to become available again. Even if the files and previous messages still exist, the reasoning process that led to the current implementation state has effectively been interrupted. For complex changes, that matters. The next run often has to reconstruct what was happening, inspect things again, understand partially completed changes and continue from there. That increases the chance of inconsistencies, regressions and unnecessary additional usage. So the limit can actually create a rather absurd cycle: Complex task → high usage → hit 5-hour limit → development interrupted → wait → Codex has to reconstruct context → additional usage → hit the limit sooner again. And all of this can happen while I still have more than 90% of my weekly quota available. I know that higher plans and additional credits exist. But “just upgrade” is not a realistic answer for everyone. I already pay for Plus, and as someone who does not have the financial means to casually spend €100–€200 every month on an AI subscription, those higher tiers simply aren’t an option for me. What frustrates me particularly is that we recently experienced how much better Codex works when the weekly allowance is the primary restriction. The temporary removal of the 5-hour limit gave users much more freedom to decide when to consume their available quota. Now the restrictive 5-hour window is back, and for my development workflow the difference is enormous. I would strongly ask OpenAI to reconsider this system. Keep the weekly limit. I have no problem with that. But either: remove the 5-hour limit again, increase it substantially, make it dynamic based on remaining weekly allowance, or allow users to consume their weekly allowance freely and throttle only when genuinely necessary. Having more than 90% of a paid weekly allowance remaining while being told that I cannot continue working for several hours simply does not feel like a sensible quota system. I really like Codex. That is exactly why this is so frustrating. I have integrated it deeply into the development of a project I genuinely care about, and right now the product is actively preventing me from using the quota that my subscription supposedly provides. But beyond my own project, I think there is a much bigger issue here. Tools like Codex have the potential to fundamentally change who gets to build software. For decades, having an idea was not enough. You also needed years of programming experience, a development team, significant funding, or access to people who had those resources. AI is changing that. Someone can have an extraordinary idea without being an extraordinary programmer. A student, an independent developer, a tiny open-source team, or simply someone sitting at home with a concept they genuinely believe could improve something can now attempt projects that previously would have required an entire company. That potential is enormous, and I believe it is necessary for the future of technology. These people should not be treated as an afterthought. I understand that enterprise customers are financially important. OpenAI is a company, compute costs money, and profitability cannot simply be ignored. But I strongly believe OpenAI should also think about what kind of ecosystem it wants its technology to create. If AI development tools primarily become practical for corporations, well-funded startups and people who can afford expensive subscriptions, we lose one of the most transformative aspects of this technology. We need independent builders. We need small teams. We need open-source developers. We need creative people who may not even know how to code yet. Their ideas do not become less valuable because they do not have a company behind them or hundreds of euros per month to spend on AI tools. In fact, some of the most interesting ideas can come precisely from people who are not constrained by corporate structures, quarterly targets, investors, risk departments or existing business models. Small independent builders can take risks that large organizations often will not. They can question established systems. They can build privacy-focused alternatives. They can create open-source projects without having to justify an immediate return on investment. They can pursue an idea simply because they believe that something should exist. TrueCord is my attempt at doing exactly that. Maybe it succeeds. Maybe it doesn’t. But I want to have the opportunity to try. And I believe there are thousands of other people with projects and ideas far beyond mine who deserve that same opportunity. This is where I think OpenAI has a responsibility that goes beyond simply selling more compute. Give those people room to build. A reasonable weekly usage allowance does that. It establishes a clear resource boundary while still allowing users to decide how they use those resources. An extremely restrictive 5-hour window does the opposite: it disproportionately hurts long, context-heavy development sessions and therefore many of the ambitious projects these tools make possible in the first place. I do not believe OpenAI should continue moving further in that direction. Even if this consequence is completely unintended, the practical result is that some of the people with the least financial and institutional resources are also the people whose ability to experiment is being restricted the most. That is not the future I want AI development tools to create. Do not only empower the companies that already have developers, capital and infrastructure. Empower the person who has nothing but an idea, a computer and the determination to build it. Because now, for the first time, that might actually be enough. And we have absolutely no idea yet what those people might create if we give them the chance.
