- 2026-08-26T14:36 johannkr7 https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/472 ::  This 5-hour limit is unacceptable — users are losing paid usage on unfinished work I want to express my strong objection to OpenAI’s decision to reintroduce the 5-hour usage limit for Plus users. This is not merely an inconvenience. It fundamentally changes the value and practical usability of a subscription that customers are already paying for. I fully understand the need for usage limits. I have no objection to a weekly compute allowance. If I consume my entire weekly allowance, that is my responsibility. But that is not what is happening here. With the new 5-hour restriction, I can be prevented from working while the vast majority of my weekly allowance is still available. I recently hit the 5-hour limit while approximately 84% of my weekly allowance was still unused . How does that make sense? If I have paid for access that includes a weekly allowance, why am I prevented from using that remaining allowance when I actually need it? Even worse is what happens to work that is already running. A task can consume a substantial amount of the 5-hour allowance and then be terminated because the limit is reached before the task has actually completed . The usage consumed by that failed or unfinished task is not returned. That means the customer can effectively pay twice for the same work: The first attempt consumes the allowance but produces no usable completed result. The customer then has to wait for the limit to reset and run the task again, consuming additional allowance simply to obtain the result that the first allocation of compute was supposed to produce. I find that completely unacceptable. If OpenAI stops a task because of its own artificial 5-hour restriction, then the usage consumed by an unfinished task should either be refunded, or the task should be allowed to continue until it reaches a successful completion or at least a safe stopping point. Anything else means customers can lose part of their paid usage through no choice of their own. From the customer’s perspective, this can feel functionally indistinguishable from having paid usage taken away: OpenAI deducts the compute, prevents the task from completing, provides no refund of the consumed allowance, and then requires the customer to spend more allowance later to finish the same job. And all of this can happen while most of the weekly allowance remains untouched . That is indefensible as a user experience. The lack of meaningful advance notice makes this even worse. Professional users build workflows around the capabilities and usage model of a paid service. OpenAI allowed users to work around a weekly allowance, giving us the flexibility to decide when during the week we needed to use our compute. Then that flexibility was suddenly taken away. Some of us do not use Work or Codex evenly throughout every day. There may be days when I barely use it at all and other days when I need to process substantial amounts of material. A weekly allowance accommodated that perfectly. A rigid 5-hour gate does not. And telling customers that the solution is effectively to move to a dramatically more expensive subscription is not an acceptable answer to a restriction imposed on an existing paid service. I am not asking OpenAI for unlimited compute. I am asking OpenAI to let paying customers use the weekly allowance they already have . At minimum, OpenAI should: Allow users to continue drawing from their remaining weekly allowance after the 5-hour allowance is exhausted. Make the 5-hour protection optional for users who prefer to manage their own weekly compute budget. Never terminate an active task solely because the 5-hour boundary has been reached. Refund usage consumed by a task if OpenAI terminates that task before completion because of a usage-limit boundary. Provide clear advance notice before materially changing the usage conditions of an existing paid subscription. Clearly show users what a task is costing while it is running so they can make an informed decision about whether to continue. The current system creates the absurd situation where a customer can have 80%+ of their weekly allowance remaining and still be unable to work . A weekly allowance that I am prevented from using when I need it is not meaningfully a weekly allowance. And compute deducted for a task that OpenAI itself prevents from completing should not simply disappear. OpenAI needs to reconsider this policy.

- 2026-08-26T17:50 linus.kemmann https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/482 ::  Feature Request: Please remove or fundamentally change the 5-hour Work/Codex limit for Plus users Hi OpenAI Team, I am a paying ChatGPT Plus subscriber and I would like to provide feedback on the recently restored 5-hour usage limit for ChatGPT Work/Codex. My issue is not that usage limits exist. I completely understand that compute-intensive models need limits, and I have no fundamental problem with having a weekly allowance. The problem is the combination of a weekly allowance and an additional restrictive 5-hour allowance. If I still have, for example, 80% of my weekly allowance remaining, I do not understand why I am prevented from using that remaining capacity when I actually need it. The 5-hour limit makes the weekly allowance less useful A weekly allowance gives the impression that I have a certain amount of Work/Codex usage available to me for the week. However, the 5-hour limit means that I cannot freely decide when to consume that allowance. In practice, the weekly allowance is therefore not really a pool of usage that I can manage myself. It is a pool that I am only allowed to consume at a predefined maximum rate. For many users, especially people working on projects, this does not reflect how real work happens. I might barely use Work for several days and then have one afternoon or evening where I want to spend several continuous hours working on a website or another larger project. That is exactly when Work is most useful. But the 5-hour restriction can stop that workflow even though I still have most of my weekly allowance available. This directly conflicts with the purpose of Work Work is particularly useful for larger, multi-step and context-heavy tasks. Those tasks naturally require iteration: implement a change inspect the result identify a problem correct it test it make another change continue developing the project This is not something that can always be divided into small sessions several hours apart. If I am actively developing a website and Work stops me after only a few substantial tasks, telling me to wait until the short-term allowance resets, the entire purpose of having an agent capable of sustained project work is undermined. The context, momentum and workflow are interrupted precisely when the feature becomes most valuable. My actual experience Today I had barely used Work. I used it for only a handful of requests, including work on a website. Some of those requests were larger tasks, but there were only approximately four major instructions. My 5-hour allowance was nevertheless exhausted extremely quickly while I still had the large majority of my weekly allowance remaining. That means I can have plenty of paid weekly capacity available and still be unable to continue a project that I am currently working on. This makes it very difficult to plan serious sessions around Work. Private Plus users should not need Pro simply to work continuously I am already paying for ChatGPT Plus every month. I am a private user rather than a company, so upgrading to a dramatically more expensive professional plan simply to remove or significantly reduce this kind of interruption is not a reasonable solution for my use case. There is a very large difference between someone who uses ChatGPT professionally at company scale and a private power user who occasionally wants to spend an entire day working intensively on a personal project. Plus should have a meaningful place between Free and very expensive professional subscriptions. If Plus users receive a weekly Work allowance, I believe they should have reasonable freedom to use that allowance when they need it. Otherwise, a heavy Plus user can effectively be rate-limited in a way that makes the practical difference between a paid account and a much more restricted usage pattern feel significantly smaller during intensive work sessions. Unused weekly allowance can effectively become wasted allowance There is another consequence that I think is important. Because the 5-hour limit prevents intensive usage, users may reach the end of their weekly period with unused weekly capacity. Not because they did not want to use Work, but because they were prevented from using enough of it at the times when they were actually available to work. For example: I may have time today to spend six or eight hours working on my website. Tomorrow I may not have time at all. If the system stops me today because of the 5-hour allowance, the fact that my weekly allowance resets later does not help me. My available time and OpenAI’s quota-reset schedule are two completely different things. From a customer’s perspective, that means I can pay for a subscription containing a weekly allowance but still be unable to make practical use of that allowance before it expires. The basic model feels backwards An analogy would be buying a pack of chewing gum but then being told that, although the entire pack belongs to you, you may only eat one piece every hour. The problem is not the number of pieces in the pack. The problem is that someone else is deciding the rate at which you are allowed to use something that has already been allocated to you. I understand that OpenAI needs to manage infrastructure load and prevent extreme usage spikes. But from the customer’s perspective, capacity planning should ideally not be solved by making a paid weekly allowance artificially difficult to consume when the user actually needs it. If the infrastructure cannot support unlimited burst usage, I would strongly prefer a system that gives users more control instead of simply stopping their workflow. Possible solutions I would strongly ask OpenAI to consider one of the following: Remove the 5-hour limit for Plus and use the weekly allowance as the primary limit. Significantly increase the 5-hour allowance , so that several hours of genuine project work are realistically possible. Allow users to choose between two modes: protected usage with a 5-hour cap for people who want to avoid accidentally exhausting their weekly allowance; unrestricted weekly-pool usage for users who deliberately want to consume their allowance faster. Allow users to override the 5-hour limit manually , with a clear warning such as: “Continuing will consume your remaining weekly allowance faster.” That would protect casual users without forcing power users into the same usage pattern. Introduce rollover or more flexible quota management , so unused capacity caused by short-term limits does not simply disappear. Provide much clearer usage information , including how much an individual Work task consumed and why. The third or fourth solution would, in my opinion, be particularly fair. If the purpose of the 5-hour restriction is to prevent users from accidentally exhausting their weekly allowance, let the user decide. Warn me. Show me the consequences. Show me my remaining weekly allowance. But if I knowingly choose to use 50%, 80% or even 100% of my weekly allowance during one intensive working day, that should ultimately be my decision. Final point I really like ChatGPT and Work is exactly the kind of feature that makes the service significantly more useful to me. That is why this limitation is so frustrating. It does not make me want to use Work more carefully. It makes me question whether I can rely on Work for larger projects at all, because I cannot know whether I will suddenly be forced to stop in the middle of a productive session despite having plenty of weekly usage remaining. Please reconsider the combination of the 5-hour and weekly limits for ChatGPT Plus users. A weekly allowance that users can manage themselves would be far more useful than an allowance that exists on paper but cannot necessarily be used when the user actually has the time and need to use it. Thank you for considering this feedback.

I suggest using the ones you agree with most. For example, if you believe the 5-hour limit should realistically be removed altogether, there is little point in emphasizing proposals that would keep it while merely increasing the allowance. One possible solution would be to make the 5-hour cap optional, enabled by default for new Plus users, while also allowing users to control what percentage of their total weekly quota may be consumed within that window. Based on many reports, it has become very difficult to make meaningful progress on something as complex as programming an application, carrying out a demanding Work task, or handling other requests that require a lot of resources over a relatively short period of time. It is also important to take into account that, from the user’s perspective, a specific programming, coding, or Work task cannot necessarily be broken down into as many small work sessions as the current 5-hour restriction effectively requires. This significantly reduces the usefulness of these features. It is equally important to take into account that people’s availability varies greatly. Many users cannot work on these projects regularly throughout the week and instead need to concentrate their work into intensive sessions whenever they have the time. The current limit prevents those users from making proper use of Work and Codex. There is also the issue of weekly allowance effectively going to waste for users who were physically unable to use their remaining weekly quota before it reset, not because they did not need or want to use it, but because the 5-hour restriction left them no practical way to do so. This can also strongly encourage Plus users to cancel their ChatGPT subscriptions and look elsewhere. Finally, as some people have pointed out, if the underlying issue is server capacity, then that capacity needs to be increased rather than making users bear the consequences of insufficient capacity. In its current form, the restriction does not merely reduce consumption: for some of the main use cases that make Work and Codex valuable, it can make the service extremely difficult to use effectively.

- 2026-08-27T08:30 ShakeShoppe https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/507 ::  Idea for Codex usage limits: treat capacity like a battery instead of a hard reset One thing that seems to frustrate a lot of Codex users is the combination of the 5-hour and 7-day usage windows. I think there may be a simpler way to make the exact same underlying compute limits feel far more intuitive: Treat Codex capacity like a rechargeable battery. You use Codex → your available capacity drops. You stop using Codex → it gradually recharges. Instead of: «“Your limit resets on Thursday at 03:17.”» You could see something like: Codex Capacity: 62% Recovering continuously ~14% capacity restored per day If the weekly allowance represents seven days of capacity, then a full recharge over 168 hours would restore roughly 0.6% per hour / 14.3% per day. You could even keep two separate capacity pools: - Burst capacity — replaces the 5-hour window and recharges quickly - Sustained capacity — replaces the weekly limit and recharges gradually This would still let OpenAI control total compute consumption and prevent extreme bursts, but it removes the strange behaviour created by fixed reset boundaries. It also changes the user experience completely. Instead of asking: «“When does my limit reset?”» The user simply asks: «“How much capacity do I have right now?”» There could even be a nonlinear recharge curve: - Low capacity → faster recovery - Mid capacity → normal recovery - Near full → slower recovery Similar to how people already understand battery charging. The underlying concept is essentially a token-bucket style rate limiter, but the UX could simply be presented as a Codex Battery or Codex Recharge system. Potential benefits: - Easier for users to understand - Less anxiety around reset dates - Rewards taking breaks naturally - Smoother demand rather than large reset cliffs - OpenAI can maintain essentially the same total compute allowance - Users always know exactly where they stand The key point is that this doesn’t necessarily require giving users more compute. It may just be a better way of distributing and communicating the compute they already have. Codex Recharge: use capacity, let it recover, keep working. Curious whether the Codex team has considered a continuously replenishing quota model rather than fixed reset windows.

When the remaining usage drops very low, the current task is interrupted. After the usage limit resets back to 100%, Codex does not reliably continue with the full context and knowledge that was already built up before the interruption. This happened to me again today. After the reset, I still had 93% usage available . Codex initially said: “I’m continuing exactly where I left off: first I’ll properly lint the recently changed cron and analysis files, then I’ll check the new logs against the cause of the ‘empty cron email’.” Shortly afterwards, it even identified a concrete cause: “This is now clear: the cron email is no longer empty. Instead, Cron Center is incorrectly loading its admin interface during execution, and that interface expects a file from the wrong module path.” The problem is that despite saying it was continuing from the previous point, Codex did not actually retain the full previous analysis state. Instead of building directly on the work that had already been completed, it started re-analyzing large parts of the project, the dependencies, the logic, and the surrounding context. As a result, after the reset I had 93% usage available , but by the time Codex had reconstructed information that had already been analyzed before, the available usage had already dropped to around 80% . That means roughly 13% of the new usage allowance was spent simply rebuilding context and repeating analysis that had already been completed before the reset . For larger development tasks, this is a serious problem. Codex may spend a significant amount of time analyzing files, dependencies, application logic, error causes, and relationships between different modules. If this knowledge is not reliably preserved when the usage limit is reached, the user effectively has to pay again for Codex to reconstruct the same context after every reset. The workflow currently looks like this: Codex analyzes a complex problem and consumes usage. The usage limit is reached. The task is interrupted. The usage limit resets. Codex says it is continuing from where it stopped. In reality, it has to re-analyze large parts of the previous context. Additional usage is consumed for work that was already done before. Only after that can the actual development work continue. Since the update, this has become one of the biggest disadvantages of using Codex for larger development tasks. The most frustrating part is that users are effectively spending usage or credits multiple times for the same work. The additional consumption does not come from new development work. It comes from Codex having to reconstruct information that it had already analyzed previously. For complex projects, debugging, and larger web development tasks, this can consume a significant part of the available usage before any new progress is actually made. I would strongly suggest improving the following: Ongoing tasks should be fully resumable after a usage-limit reset. The previously built analysis and project context should be preserved. Files, dependencies, and relationships that were already analyzed should not need to be analyzed again. The exact working state should be saved when a task is interrupted because of the usage limit. After the reset, Codex should continue from that exact state instead of reconstructing the project context. Users should not have to spend additional usage rebuilding context that was already processed and paid for. For short tasks, this issue may not be very noticeable. For large development projects, however, it makes Codex significantly less efficient and much more expensive to use because a noticeable percentage of each new usage allowance can be lost simply repeating previous analysis.

At approximately 2:26 AM: - 5-hour allowance: 84% remaining - weekly allowance: approximately 81% At approximately 3:59 AM: - 5-hour allowance: 0% - weekly allowance: still approximately 68% That is only about: 1 hour 33 minutes of wall-clock time. And yes, this was heavy work. The agent was dealing with Live state, dependencies, role bindings, worldbook structure, readback verification, checkpoints, document integration, and validation. But that is exactly why I selected Sol High. I am not using a high-end reasoning model to rename files. If complex integration work is precisely where the high-end model becomes difficult to use continuously, when am I supposed to use it? -– Case 3: 19% → 1% in about 12 minutes, while weekly usage was still 41% On another day: At approximately 8:51 PM: - 5-hour allowance: 19% - weekly allowance: 44% At approximately 9:03 PM: - 5-hour allowance: 1% - weekly allowance: 41% The next remote run immediately failed with: “You’ve hit your usage limit.” The UI then offered: - Upgrade to Pro - Purchase more credits - Wait for the next reset But again: I still had 41% of my included weekly allowance remaining. This is the part I find hardest to accept. -– If my weekly allowance is exhausted, I will buy credits I am not opposed to paying for compute. A reasonable relationship is easy to understand: included weekly usage exhausted → project still needs more compute → buy additional credits Fine. But what I am currently seeing is: weekly still 84% → short window exhausted weekly still 68% → short window exhausted weekly still 41% → short window exhausted → UI suggests Upgrade / Purchase credits At that point, I no longer know what I am buying. Am I buying: additional compute? Or am I paying for: the ability to keep working right now, while a large amount of compute already included in my subscription is still sitting unused? Those are psychologically very different purchases. And ironically, this behavior makes me less willing to purchase credits, not more. -– I was already considering Pro — this change is making me hesitate I also think this matters for users who are still learning how to use AI agents effectively. I do not think the responsible first step for a new agent user is necessarily: buy the largest token package immediately. Agent workflows take time to learn. I have been deliberately improving how I work: - deciding what actually needs a high-end model - moving cheaper work to lower-cost models - managing context - creating checkpoints - avoiding repeated reads - externalizing snapshots - splitting work into sensible stages - measuring my actual weekly consumption In other words: learn how to use the agent efficiently first, then decide how much compute I actually need. That does not mean I am unwilling to pay. Quite the opposite. Before this 5-hour behavior became a problem, I was already seriously considering upgrading to Pro because my project genuinely needs more usage. Now I hesitate. Because I cannot answer the most basic question: Will upgrading actually solve the bottleneck I am hitting? If Pro simply gives me more total or weekly usage, but the same short-window structure can still exhaust itself before one integration cycle finishes, I may just be paying more money for: more fragmented usage. I do not need the phrase “more usage.” I need to know: How much more continuous usable capacity do I actually get? -– The irony is that higher-end business offerings already recognize the value of removing the 5-hour limit I understand product segmentation. Enterprise and business customers paying more should receive more capability. But that makes the current individual-plan experience even more noticeable. Higher-end business offerings can explicitly advertise: “no five-hour usage limit.” So clearly, the ability to perform substantial work without repeatedly hitting this short-window barrier has product value. For individual users, then, I think the product should clearly distinguish between: - more weekly total - more 5-hour capacity - more continuous usable capacity - or all of the above Those are not the same thing. Especially when the UI blocks work with 41% weekly usage remaining, then immediately presents: Upgrade to Pro / Purchase more credits A potential customer naturally asks: If I upgrade, does the wall I am actually hitting move? Without that information, how am I supposed to make an informed purchase decision? -– I have a full-time job. I am not a full-time vibe coder. This may be the most important real-world part of the problem. I have a full-time job. I work roughly 22 days per month. This project is not my job. I am creating something in my private time. The hours when I can do substantial AI-assisted work are usually just a few hours after work at night. That is one of the reasons I use AI agents: I want to exchange compute for time. I do not want to exchange my private time for quota management. But my evenings are increasingly becoming: check the 5-hour allowance → decide whether I dare submit the full instruction → worry whether a global audit is too expensive → deliberately ask Sol to do less → check the next reset time → decide whether to wait for another window → check context → check external sessions → decide whether to switch models → decide where I need another checkpoint I already spend most weekdays working. How many times is one creative project supposed to be sliced apart by short quota windows? And what practical value does a large remaining weekly allowance have if I cannot actually use it during the limited hours when I am free? I do not lack weekly compute. I lack a usable block of time in which I can actually consume it. If using AI means I have to spend my limited evening hours asking: “Do I dare give the model the full instruction right now?” then at some point I have to ask: Would continuing to use my own brain simply save more time? -– My workflow is not one prompt. It is a feedback loop. This is not: prompt → answer → done It is closer to: read current Live state → compare against existing structure → reason about global dependencies → modify → read back → test → identify problems → modify again → verify again In other words: integration → test → correction → integration The difficult iterative stage is still ahead. If even one relatively small stage cannot reliably reach closure before the short window interrupts it, how is the later “test while modifying” phase supposed to work? And I have already been deliberately conservative: - lower-cost model for retrieval - snapshots saved externally - checkpoints externalized - repeated reads reduced - tasks split - canonical documents saved every round - I deliberately did not even ask Sol to perform the full global logic / integration audit yet And the workflow still repeatedly hits the wall. At some point, it stops feeling like: the user is using an agent and starts feeling like: the user is managing the agent’s quota scheduler. -– External MCP sessions make “just wait for the next window” expensive Part of my workflow involves an external MCP with a limited session lifetime. This is not a large enterprise API designed for permanent unattended server-to-server automation. In my current setup, establishing or re-establishing the connection requires a human to: use the computer → operate the relevant program / terminal → open the browser → complete the authorization flow → confirm the connection The AI cannot autonomously complete that step. And I do not necessarily consider that a flaw. Human authorization is currently part of the security boundary. I have already started thinking about whether controlled Chrome automation could someday assist with MCP verification safely. But I do not think I should be forced to suddenly redesign a reasonable security workflow simply to accommodate OpenAI’s quota window. The intended workflow is: human establishes MCP → agent works during the valid session → human leaves the computer → monitors or continues remotely → agent completes a meaningful stage Instead, the quota system can turn that into: human establishes MCP → agent works → OpenAI short window reaches zero → wait several hours → MCP session is near expiration or expired → human must return to the computer and authorize again So “wait for the next 5-hour window” is not free. It destroys part of the usable external-tool session. -– If this does not improve, I will seriously consider moving my “thinking relay station” This is not meant as: “Fix this or I rage-quit.” It is simply a time-cost calculation. I am going to try to finish the remaining stages of this project. But if I manage to finish it through all these constraints and the short-window behavior still has not meaningfully improved, then I will have to seriously ask: Should OpenAI continue to be my primary agent execution environment and thinking relay station? Because instead of continuing to spend mental energy on: quota → resets → model routing → context → whether I dare submit the full task → when the next window arrives I can spend that same mental energy researching: other models, other agents, and other long-running execution environments. Fortunately, I had enough foresight not to keep my project exclusively inside one AI platform. My important project state is stored both locally and in: GitHub. Canonical sources, checkpoints, version history, and important workflow state are externally preserved. Originally that was simply good version control and backup practice. Now I realize it is also: portability. So the real question keeping me on a platform is not: “Can I move?” It is: “Which assistant actually requires the least effort to manage the assistant itself?” I am still taking the time to document all of this because I actually want to continue using OpenAI. I was even considering paying more. But if the result becomes: struggle to finish this project → 5-hour problem remains → next project requires managing the exact same quota windows again then my next investment of mental energy may simply go toward evaluating other tools. My private time is limited. If I have to spend brainpower anyway, I would rather spend it learning a new tool than repeatedly learning how to work around the same quota bucket. -– What I actually want OpenAI to change Again, I am not asking for unlimited Sol. My requests are much simpler. 1. Let users choose whether they need the 5-hour protective gate Users who want protection against accidentally burning through weekly usage can keep it. Power users who are willing to manage their own weekly allowance should be able to opt into something like: weekly-first / burst mode If I knowingly choose to spend a large portion of my weekly allowance in one evening, let that be my decision. -– 2. Provide per-run usage breakdowns I should be able to understand approximately: - which run - which model - which workload consumed how much of my included usage. Instead of seeing: 19% → 1% → 0% and having to reverse-engineer what happened. -– 3. Clearly explain what higher plans actually increase I need to know whether a higher plan increases: - weekly total - 5-hour capacity - continuous usable capacity - or all of them If Pro still has a short-window gate, a potential upgrader should be able to understand: how much more short-window capacity do I actually get? Otherwise the only way to find out is: pay first → benchmark it myself afterward That is not a good basis for an upgrade decision. -– 4. If weekly allowance is not exhausted, additional payment should not be the main way to preserve workflow continuity If my weekly allowance is exhausted: I will buy credits. But if I still have: 41%, 68%, or 84% and the short window repeatedly blocks work before suggesting: Purchase more credits then users will naturally ask: Am I buying more compute, or am I buying permission to work right now? That ambiguity discourages spending from users who were otherwise willing to pay. -– Final point A rate limit should limit: how much compute I can use. It should not effectively mean: “You still have a large amount of included compute, but you are not allowed to use it now.” If I actually exhaust my weekly allowance: I will buy credits. If transparent data shows that my workload genuinely requires a higher plan and that upgrading actually addresses the bottleneck I am hitting: I will upgrade. I was already considering Pro. This change is what made me hesitate. Because what I want to buy is: more compute and more usable working time. Not: more quota buckets that I have to personally manage after work. I use AI agents because I want less babysitting. I do not want a second job as an: AI quota manager. Evidence attached: multiple 5-hour windows exhausting while substantial weekly allowance remained. - 2026-08-28T01:57 sebastien1a https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/533 ::  (Original French message below) As for me, I’ve just finished writing and sending the following message to OpenAI Support. Please avoid simply copy-pasting it, but if it can give some of you ideas… There’s nothing new compared with what has already been said here, but it roughly summarizes the main points, while possibly leaving out some issues raised by some of you: BEGINNING OF THE MESSAGE I SENT AFTER EXPLICITLY ASKING FOR A HUMAN SUPPORT AGENT TO HANDLE IT AND ESCALATE IT INTERNALLY REGARDING THE RECENT 5-HOUR LIMITS FOR CHATGPT PLUS USERS WITH WORK/CODEX Hello. I am writing regarding the recent reinstatement of the 5-hour window and its associated limits for ChatGPT Plus users with their Work/Codex quota. Initially, I was only planning to write on my own behalf, but after reading the many strong reactions on the OpenAI Community Forum in addition to the ones I posted myself, I really encourage you to carefully read the different detailed experiences, the complaints about the problems caused by the reinstated 5-hour limit, and the various alternatives that many of us are asking you to consider. Here is the relevant discussion thread: https://community.openai.com/t/codex-rate-limits-discussion-thread/1378553/521 I am now using this channel to ask you to escalate internally my observations, my complaint about what I would describe here as a regression, and especially my suggestions as well as those from other users that you can read through the link above. These are not insignificant, as they come from loyal and regular users of the services you provide. Regarding my recent experience since the 5-hour limits were reinstated for Plus users: I have been using Codex for some time with the Sol model at High or Very High reasoning, and while I understand the arguments given for this limit — preventing inexperienced users from wasting their quota without realizing it, and avoiding overloading the servers — I do not think these reasons are sufficient, and I believe other alternatives could achieve the same goals. First of all, the issue for paying users, as many people have pointed out, is the level of control they have over how they use their weekly quota. We are paying for that quota, so it seems legitimate to be able to use it as we see fit. If that creates too much load, intermediate solutions exist, and I include some of them among my suggestions below. In my case, with my current Codex project — creating a Windows application that must be accessible with screen readers such as JAWS — I reach the 5-hour window limit very quickly. When I check my weekly quota usage once the 5-hour allowance has reached 0, I have used barely 16% of my weekly quota. This is both frustrating and excessive as a safeguard. In addition, the work window seems extremely limited to me, not only for longer and more substantial projects, but also for carrying out different tasks that require more reasoning, such as solving a computer, coding, or other technical problem. It leaves users very little room to work, and one or two prompts can be enough to consume the entire 5-hour window without even being able to finish the task that was started. This makes it very difficult to take proper advantage of the main strengths of Work and Codex, which are precisely long-running uninterrupted work and more complex tasks that require moving between different specialized tools. These problems become even more significant because, with such a limited work window, resuming context after an interruption can sometimes consume as much or even more quota, because the model has to determine where it left off, handle more context, work out what has already been completed and what has not, what remains to be done, and deal with many other issues described in detail on the Community Forum with concrete examples. I would also add that a user may only be available to work on substantial Work/Codex projects at certain specific times during the week. If their work window is restricted in addition to the weekly quota, this can make it extremely difficult or even impossible for them to make progress on their Work or Codex projects. Many people have work obligations during the week, while others have other commitments that take up most of their time, so they may concentrate their efforts into more intensive sessions on weekends or on days when they are not working. This is one of the most recurring complaints raised in the discussion thread mentioned above. For users who cannot distribute their work evenly throughout the week, or who need longer continuous tasks — which clearly applies to many regular OpenAI users — the fact that the 5-hour usage limit represents only a small portion of the total weekly quota means that part of their weekly quota ends up unused and therefore wasted, not because they did not want or need to use it, but because they simply do not have a practical opportunity to make proper use of it. I therefore think there are several possible intermediate measures compared with the current system: Allow users to manually choose what percentage of their weekly quota they are willing to consume within 5 hours, possibly with a cap such as 40% or 50% of the total allowance. This would make it possible to carry out more complex and resource-intensive agentic tasks without disruptive interruptions, while still limiting the load on OpenAI’s servers for as long as they are not able to handle more. It would also make new users more responsible for deciding how quickly they are willing to use up their quota. If the option above is not possible, at least significantly increase the usage limit within the 5-hour window. This would allow more of the common tasks currently generating the strongest negative reactions to be completed more cleanly, even though it would not solve the problem for the many users who need intensive sessions at specific days and times of the week. Offer an intermediate paid subscription between Plus and Pro, with increased usage limits and either no 5-hour limit or full control over the 5-hour usage limit. This would also address the needs of users who require more quota than Plus provides without forcing them to move to something five times more expensive, which many cannot afford, even though they might be willing to pay more for higher and more flexible limits. I would also like to point out that credits are not a viable option for many of us, both based on user feedback and on my own experience: their cost compared with the amount of work they allow is neither cost-effective nor financially sustainable. This is not about asking for more compute than can be provided to us, but about allowing us to use our weekly quota according to our needs rather than imposing additional limits that are so restrictive that many users are considering cancelling their subscriptions and moving to competitors. According to reports from some of the users most affected, some have already done so. Introduce a system that either warns users when they are consuming a large amount of quota in a short period of time and leaves the decision in their hands, or temporarily blocks further quota consumption until the current 5-hour window ends while giving them an option, from the task currently in progress, to unlock continued Work/Codex usage. This would allow users who are not yet used to monitoring their quota to take it into account, and it could reduce the load on OpenAI’s servers because some users would respond to the warning by postponing further agentic usage. Provide an option to enable or disable the 5-hour limit, enabled by default for new Plus users so that they are aware of their usage and can then decide for themselves whether they still want to continue. This would address the very recurring request within the OpenAI user community to be able to manage one’s own agentic usage limits — in other words, to have reasonable overall limits while deciding personally how to distribute them throughout the week. This would also allow users who do not have time for many short work sessions to prioritize intensive sessions on certain days. At the very least, when users reach a 5-hour usage limit, before blocking the continuation of a task already in progress, automatically send an instruction to the agent asking it to stop or finish what it is doing cleanly, so that data is not lost and work is not left partially completed in a state that could later be lost. At the time I am writing and sending this message, Luna Reserve has just appeared on some accounts, but Luna does not have sufficient capabilities for many of the tasks that caused users to hit the limit in the first place — generally, those users needed the tools and capabilities of Sol at High reasoning or above. It therefore cannot reliably stop or complete the current task cleanly, and there are already reports to that effect in the discussion thread linked at the beginning of this message. It is even less of a viable option for continuing complex tasks that have already been started or that require specific tools which Sol is much better equipped to handle. I therefore want to make clear that I fully share the concern and frustration expressed by the many users who have posted in the dedicated OpenAI Community Forum thread, and I renew my request for all of these observations, comments, and especially suggestions to be escalated internally, in the hope that they will be reviewed and taken seriously given the number of regular users proposing these alternatives and, above all, being negatively affected by the current system. END OF MESSAGE En ce qui me concerne, je viens de terminer de rédiger puis d’envoyer le message suivant au support d’OpenAI, merci d’éviter les copier coller mais si ça peut en inspirer certains… rien de neuf par rapport à ce qui a déjà été dit, mais grossomodo synthétisé tout en oubliant possiblement des points soulevés par certains d’entre vous : DÉBUT DU MESSAGE TRANSMIS APRÈS AVOIR EXPLICITEMENT DEMANDÉ LA PRISE EN CHARGE PAR UN HUMAIN POUR TRANSMISSION EN INTERNE AU SUJET DES LIMITES RÉCENTES DE 5 HEURES AVEC WORK/CODEX POUR LES UTILISATEURS + DE CHATGPT Bonjour. Je vous écris au sujet du récent rétablissement de la fenêtre de 5 heures avec des limites associées pour les utilisateurs de ChatGPT + avec leur quota Work/Codex. Initialement, je comptais ne vous écrire qu’en mon nom, mais en lisant les nombreuses vives réactions sur le forum communautaire d’OpenAI en plus de celles que j’ai postées, je vous invite à vraiment lire attentivement les différentes expériences détaillées, les réclamations quant aux problèmes provoqués par la limite réinstaurée de 5 heures, et les différentes éventualités que nous sommes nombreux à vous demander d’envisager. Voici le fil de discussion concerné Codex Rate Limits Discussion Thread (2) À présent je passe par le présent canal pour vous demander de rapporter en interne mes observations, ma plainte par rapport à ce que je qualifierai ici de régression, en particulier mes suggestions et toutes celles des autres utilisateurs que vous pourrez lire via le lien ci-dessus, non négligeable puisqu’il s’agit d’utilisateurs fidèles et réguliers des services que vous proposez. Concernant mon expérience récente suite au rétablissement des limites de 5 heures pour les utilisateurs + : j’utilise depuis quelques temps Codex avec le modèle Sol élevé ou très élevé et si je comprends les arguments donnés pour la limite en question (éviter aux utilisateurs inexpérimentés de gaspiller leur quota sans s’en apercevoir et éviter de surcharger les serveurs), je trouve qu’ils ne sont pas suffisants et que d’autres alternatives sont envisageables pour répondre aux mêmes objectifs. Tout d’abord, le souci pour les utilisateurs qui paient est, comme beaucoup l’ont souligné, le niveau de contrôle dont ils disposent sur l’utilisation de leur quota hebdomadaire à leur guise. On paie pour cela, il paraît donc légitime de l’utiliser comme on l’entend ; si cela représente une charge trop élevée, des solutions intermédiaires existent et je reprends certaines d’entre elles parmi mes suggestions ci-après. Pour ma part, avec mon projet en cours avec Codex (création d’une application Windows qui doit être accessible aux lecteurs d’écran tels que Jaws), j’atteins la limite de la fenêtre de 5 heures en très peu de temps, et quand je consulte mon quota hebdomadaire utilisé alors que celui de 5 heures est à 0, j’ai consommé à peine 16 % de mon quota hebdomadaire. C’est à la fois frustrant et excessif comme mesure de sécurité. En outre, cela me paraît extrêmement réduit comme fenêtre de travail, non seulement pour les longs projets qui sont plus conséquents, mais aussi pour effectuer différentes tâches nécessitant plus de raisonnement comme la résolution d’un problème informatique, de code ou autre. Cela laisse vraiment peu de marge de manœuvre pour l’utilisateur et un à deux prompts peuvent suffire à consommer l’entièreté de la fenêtre de 5 heures sans même avoir pu terminer la tâche lancée. Cela rend très difficilement exploitables les principaux atouts de Work et Codex qui sont précisément le travail de longue durée ininterrompu et les tâches plus complexes nécessitant de jongler entre différents outils spécifiques. Ces problèmes prennent d’autant plus d’ampleur qu’avec une fenêtre de travail aussi limitée, la reprise du contexte par le modèle après interruption consomme parfois autant voire plus de quota parce qu’il doit retrouver là où il en était, gérer davantage de contexte, faire la part entre ce qui a déjà été fait ou non et ce qu’il reste à faire, et bien d’autres problèmes détaillés sur le forum communautaire qui reprennent ces points par des exemples détaillés. J’ajouterais qu’un utilisateur peut n’être disponible pour travailler sur des projets conséquents avec Work/Codex qu’à certains moments bien précis de la semaine et que s’il est limité dans sa fenêtre de travail en plus du quota hebdomadaire, cela peut rendre extrêmement difficile voire impossible pour lui d’avancer dans ses projets avec Work ou Codex. De nombreuses personnes ayant des obligations professionnelles pendant la semaine, ou d’autres utilisateurs ayant des impératifs occupant une majeure partie de leur temps, peuvent par exemple concentrer leurs efforts sur des sessions plus intensives le week-end ou les jours où elles ne travaillent pas. Cela fait partie des plaintes les plus récurrentes formulées dans le fil de discussion mentionné précédemment. En lien avec les utilisateurs ne pouvant pas répartir leur travail de façon égale dans la semaine ou ayant besoin de tâches continues plus longues (ce qui représente manifestement de nombreux utilisateurs réguliers d’OpenAI), vu que la limite d’utilisation en 5 heures ne représente qu’une petite part du quota hebdomadaire total, une partie de leur quota hebdomadaire se retrouve inutilisé et donc gaspillé, non pas parce qu’ils ne souhaitaient pas s’en servir ou n’en avaient pas l’utilité, mais parce qu’ils n’ont pas la possibilité matérielle de l’exploiter correctement. Ensuite, je pense qu’il y a des mesures intermédiaires possibles par rapport au fonctionnement actuel : Pouvoir choisir manuellement quel pourcentage de notre quota hebdomadaire on est disposé à consommer en 5 heures, avec éventuellement un plafond comme 40 ou 50 % de l’utilisation totale. Cela permet d’effectuer des tâches agentiques plus complexes et plus coûteuses sans interruptions intempestives tout en limitant la charge pour les serveurs d’OpenAI tant qu’ils ne seront pas en mesure de l’assumer. De plus, cela responsabilise les nouveaux utilisateurs quant à la vitesse à laquelle ils sont prêts à épuiser leur quota. À défaut de l’option ci-dessus, au moins augmenter de façon significative la limite d’utilisation dans la fenêtre de 5 heures. Cela permettrait à davantage de tâches courantes qui suscitent le plus de réactions négatives actuelles de se faire plus proprement même si cela ne résoudrait pas le problème de la nécessité pour un grand nombre d’effectuer des sessions intensives à des jours et moments précis de la semaine. Proposer un abonnement payant intermédiaire entre le + et le pro avec des limites d’utilisation accrues et la disparition ou la possibilité de contrôler entièrement sa limite d’utilisation en 5 heures. Cela répondrait également aux demandes des utilisateurs ayant besoin de plus de quota que n’en offre l’abonnement + sans passer à quelque chose de 5 fois plus cher alors que beaucoup n’en ont pas les moyens alors qu’ils pourraient payer davantage pour des limites plus élevées et flexibles. Je précise que les crédits ne sont pas une option viable pour beaucoup d’entre nous, à la fois d’après des retours utilisateurs et d’après mon expérience, leur coût par rapport à la quantité de travail qu’ils permettent n’est ni rentable, ni soutenable financièrement. Il ne s’agit d’ailleurs pas de demander plus de puissance de calcul que l’on ne peut nous en fournir, mais de nous permettre d’utiliser notre quota hebdomadaire en fonction de nos besoins et non avec des limites supplémentaires si contraignantes que de nombreux utilisateurs songent à résilier leur abonnement et aller à la concurrence, certains l’ont déjà fait d’après ce qu’on peut lire dans les témoignages des personnes les plus touchées. Mettre en place un système soit avertissant l’utilisateur quand il utilise beaucoup de quota en peu de temps et le laissant maître de ce qu’il en fait, soit bloquant la consommation du reste de son quota jusqu’à la fin des 5 heures entamées mais en lui laissant l’option depuis la tâche en cours de débloquer la poursuite de ses tâches Work/Codex. Cela permettrait aux utilisateurs qui n’ont pas encore l’habitude de surveiller leur quota d’en tenir compte, et cela pourrait réduire la charge pour les serveurs d’OpenAI dans la mesure où un certain nombre d’utilisateurs tiendraient compte de cet avertissement et remettraient à plus tard la consommation du reste de leur quota agentique. Avoir une option activant ou désactivant la limite des 5 heures activée par défaut pour les nouveaux utilisateurs +, afin qu’ils aient conscience de ce qu’ils consomment et puissent eux-mêmes décider s’ils souhaitent poursuivre malgré tout. Cela répondrait à la demande très récurrente dans la communauté constituée par les utilisateurs des services d’OpenAI consistant à pouvoir gérer soi-même sa limite d’utilisation agentique, donc tout en ayant des limites raisonnables, décider soi-même de comment on la répartit sur la semaine. Cela aurait l’avantage de permettre à ceux n’ayant pas le temps de faire beaucoup de petites sessions de travail de privilégier des sessions intensives certains jours. Au grand minimum pour les utilisateurs atteignant une limite d’utilisation de 5 heures, avant le blocage de la poursuite d’une tâche en cours, envoyer un message automatique à l’agent pour lui demander de terminer proprement ce qu’il est en train de faire, afin de ne pas perdre des données ou avoir un travail partiellement achevé qui pourrait être perdu. Au moment où je rédige et transmets ce message, Luna Reserve vient d’apparaître sur certains comptes, mais le modèle Luna ne disposant pas des capacités suffisantes pour de nombreuses tâches qui ont suscité le dépassement de limite (généralement les utilisateurs avaient besoin des outils de Sol élevé ou davantage), il ne sait pas interrompre ou terminer proprement la tâche en cours, des témoignages existent déjà en ce sens sur le fil de discussion dont le lien est en début du présent message. Il s’agit encore moins d’une option viable pour poursuivre des tâches complexes entamées ou nécessitant des outils précis que Sol est bien davantage à même de pouvoir gérer. Je tiens donc à préciser que je partage pleinement l’inquiétude et la frustration des nombreux utilisateurs qui se sont exprimés sur le forum communautaire d’OpenAI dans le fil de discussion dédié, et je renouvelle ma demande pour que l’ensemble de ces observations, commentaires et surtout suggestions soient transmis en interne, dans l’espoir que ces suggestions seront examinées et prises au sérieux vu le nombre d’utilisateurs réguliers proposant ces alternatives et surtout étant négativement impactés par le fonctionnement actuel. FIN DU MESSAGE

- 2026-08-28T05:15 ramiro.riquelme https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/538 ::  I would like to raise a concern about the current usage-limit structure for ChatGPT Plus. I understand why OpenAI needs usage limits. What I do not understand is why Plus users need both a weekly quota and an additional restrictive 5-hour window. The problem is straightforward: I can still have a large percentage of my weekly allowance available and nevertheless be unable to continue working because I have reached the 5-hour limit. For sustained professional use, this is extremely disruptive. A weekly quota is reasonable because it allows users to distribute their usage according to their own workflow. Some people work a little every day; others need several hours of intensive work on one or two days. A short-window cap removes that flexibility. This matters especially for research, coding, document analysis, academic writing, data analysis, and other long sessions where continuity is important. Reaching a temporary 5-hour cap while most of the weekly quota is still unused makes the weekly allowance feel largely artificial. My objection is therefore not “Plus should be unlimited.” The issue is the interaction between two different limits. A more sensible system would be one of the following: - Keep the weekly quota but remove the 5-hour cap. - Allow users to continue consuming their weekly allocation after reaching the short-window limit. - Make the short-window allowance substantially larger. - Introduce a flexible or burst-usage system for users who concentrate their work into longer sessions. For a paid productivity tool, users should be able to decide when to consume their available weekly capacity. I would be interested to know whether other Plus users are encountering the same situation: significant weekly usage remaining, but work interrupted by the 5-hour limit. If this limit was recently introduced or tightened, greater transparency from OpenAI about the reasoning and the actual quota structure would also be appreciated. 
- 2026-08-28T05:40 merefield https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/539 ::  ramiro.riquelme: What I do not understand is why Plus users need both a weekly quota and an additional restrictive 5-hour window. System availability. It’s to limit the chance that users will overwhelm the system. By having a five hour limit, you are forcing users to spread more load over the week more evenly, thereby reducing usage spikes and giving the infrastructure more chance to keep up and therefore maintaining system responsiveness and service for everyone. It’s not a marketing-driven product class limitation, it’s an engineering safeguard.

- 2026-08-28T08:25 logremonte https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/544 ::  Before the 5-hour limit was introduced, the weekly limit still allowed me to use Codex productively on large, multi-stage projects. I could plan the work, implement changes, run tests, fix issues, perform regression checks, and actually finish a meaningful stage without constantly watching a short-term usage counter. Since the additional 5-hour limit was introduced, that has changed dramatically. And at this point, the problem is no longer just that the 5-hour limit is too restrictive. The quality and reliability of Codex itself also appear to have regressed significantly. I am using GPT-5.6 Sol with “Very High” reasoning on a fairly large production WordPress plugin project, so I have a good basis for comparison with how Codex behaved before. Right now, Codex can consume a surprisingly large amount of usage on tasks that it previously handled efficiently. Some examples from actual project work: On relatively small changes, it can spend many minutes analyzing or “designing” a solution instead of simply making the requested change. After hitting the usage limit and resuming later, it sometimes loses track of the current project state and starts revisiting work that had already been tested and closed. We had to introduce dedicated checkpoint files just to prevent it from effectively restarting its reasoning after an interruption. After resuming, it has sometimes forgotten which tests had already passed and wanted to run them all over again for no meaningful reason. There have been cases where it misunderstood relatively simple layout requirements, introduced another problem while fixing the first one, and then spent another iteration fixing the problem it had just created. There have also been situations where Codex reported something as PASS , but manual verification showed that the actual requirement had not been met and we had to reopen the task. We eventually had to introduce an additional procedure where, before real local WordPress testing, we compare file paths, SHA-256 hashes and file sizes between the project and the local runtime just to make sure Codex is actually testing the current code. When the 5-hour limit is exhausted, the entire workflow is interrupted at an arbitrary point. Then you wait for the reset, return to the project, and spend part of the next allowance recovering context and correcting Codex’s own previous mistakes. The result is a ridiculous feedback loop: Codex makes a mistake → consumes the limit → fixes its own mistake → consumes more limit → gets interrupted → loses part of the context → re-analyzes work that was already completed. That is especially frustrating because this was much less of a problem when there was only the weekly limit. The weekly limit still allowed complicated projects to move forward in a sensible way. With the current 5-hour limit, even a normal series of regression or security tests can become awkward to complete in one uninterrupted session. I have also noticed that, since the 5-hour limit was introduced, the weekly allowance seems to be disappearing faster as well . Obviously I do not have visibility into how OpenAI calculates usage internally, so I am presenting this strictly as a user observation. But the difference in the amount of useful work I can get done before the limits become a problem is very noticeable. Unfortunately, I am seeing a similar regression in ChatGPT itself when working on long-running projects. It appears to lose previously established project context more often, even when that information exists in the project history and checkpoints. I increasingly have to remind it about an existing backlog, current release state, or rules that were already established earlier. A recent example with Codex was particularly absurd. I tried to give it a larger instruction for a final regression and security audit. The interface got stuck on “Adding pasted text…” . Nothing was clickable, refreshing did not help, and I eventually had to completely close and restart Codex. After restarting it, the instruction was simply gone. The audit had never started. I had to create a new chat branch and rewrite the prompt in a shorter form just to continue working. So for me this is no longer simply a debate about whether “5 hours is enough.” Something appears to have regressed across the overall experience: stability, context retention, reasoning efficiency, error rate, and the amount of quota required to accomplish the same amount of useful work. For a tool intended for serious software development, that matters a lot. If I have to supervise Codex constantly, stop it when it spends too long over-analyzing a small task, verify that it is testing the correct files, double-check whether its PASS really means PASS, maintain my own recovery checkpoints in case it loses context, and then wait for a usage reset after it spent a significant portion of the allowance correcting its own mistakes, the value proposition starts to fall apart. The weekly limit was workable. The current 5-hour limit, combined with the apparent regression in quality and stability, makes Codex increasingly impractical for larger projects. I hope OpenAI treats this as a technical/product-quality issue rather than simply feedback from a user asking for a higher limit. A larger limit would certainly help, but a larger limit alone will not solve the underlying problem if Codex continues spending that allowance on repeated work, self-correction, lost context, unnecessary analysis, and avoidable mistakes.

- 2026-08-28T13:48 AleVen82 https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/557 ::  I have already submitted this feedback directly to OpenAI Support, which confirmed that it has been logged as product feedback and will be routed to the Codex team for review. My concern is not the existence of usage limits. I understand that OpenAI needs to manage capacity, costs, and fair use. The issue is how the current limits interact. When a significant amount of weekly capacity is still available, a short-term 5-hour limit should protect against exceptional bursts without unnecessarily preventing legitimate concentrated use. At the moment, however, the combination of the 5-hour window and the weekly limit can create a situation where a user still has substantial weekly capacity available but cannot continue working when that capacity is actually needed. For professional and complex workflows, this matters because usage is not necessarily uniform throughout the week. Some days may require very little Codex usage, while a difficult task may require several hours of concentrated work. This is especially important because Codex is an agent, not simply a prompt-response interface. A complex task may involve reading a repository, reconstructing context, planning changes, editing multiple files, running tests, identifying failures, and correcting them. If the short-term limit is reached during that process, the task may stop before reaching a safe or useful completion point. Resuming later may also require additional context reconstruction, creating further overhead. So the problem is not simply “I want more usage.” The problem is predictability, flexibility, and continuity of work within the capacity that OpenAI already considers acceptable for the account. Possible approaches could include: removing the 5-hour limit and using the weekly quota as the primary constraint; significantly increasing the short-term allowance; allowing greater short-term usage when substantial weekly capacity remains; making the short-term protection configurable or replacing a hard stop with warnings at appropriate thresholds; using a progressively replenishing capacity model instead of rigid reset windows; when the short-term limit is reached, preventing new tasks from starting while allowing an already-running task to reach a safe completion point. The last point is particularly important for agentic workflows. A rate limit should ideally prevent additional consumption without leaving an active task in an arbitrary intermediate state. I am not asking for unlimited usage, nor am I arguing against fair-use controls. I am asking for a rate-limiting system that controls both total consumption and exceptional bursts while still allowing users to plan their work, make reasonable use of available capacity, and complete tasks safely. I also believe that material changes to these limits should be communicated clearly, including how the different limits interact and what practical impact users should expect on each plan. The core principle is simple: Rate limits should control resource consumption without unnecessarily controlling when legitimate work can happen or interrupting work already in progress.

- 2026-08-29T00:18 PaulBellow https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/574 ::  Topic summary The thread began after Codex moved from message-based to token-based usage and its 2× promotion ended. VeitB explained that model choice, prompt/repository/context size, AGENTS.md , MCP servers, Fast mode, and images can accelerate consumption; later he confirmed that the reduction was real, with Business limits adjusted further while unexpected Plus/Pro reductions were under investigation ( post 95 ). Many users report that workflows which previously lasted days now exhaust the “5-hour” allowance in minutes or 2–7 prompts. Examples include three focused passes consuming an entire window , one request taking 67% , and Business accounts draining much faster than Plus for comparable work ( post 218 ). Teams with annual subscriptions say the abrupt change disrupted established automation, exhausted purchased credits, and made costs unpredictable. Others argue that subsidized inference was inevitably going to become more expensive, but critics emphasize that the primary problem is abrupt implementation, unclear communication, and loss of trust rather than the existence of limits. Users proposed several explanations: repeated long-context transmission, disproportionate token-to-quota conversion, cached-token or subagent double-counting, incorrect quota buckets, and hidden penalties for multiple accounts. The multi-account theory remained unconfirmed and was challenged by tests on previously unused accounts ( post 145 ). Later diagnostics identified possible background consumption: one Pro user found codex-auto-review represented roughly 75% of locally logged usage ( post 391 ), while another traced unexpected API spending to a VS Code extension silently using OPENAI_API_KEY ; signing out and back in restored expected behavior ( post 341 ). OpenAI Support clarified that usage is not based simply on prompt count or wall-clock time. It depends on tokens, model, task/context size, local versus cloud execution, Fast mode, compactions, tools, and background work such as reviews, automations, subagents, terminals, and Memories. Users should inspect Usage & billing , /status , /memories , and active background tasks. Suspected accounting problems should be submitted to support@openai.com with plan, model, execution mode, timestamp/timezone, screenshots, and enabled background features; earlier support also requested a HAR file ( post 72 ). Common requests include transparent per-task/source accounting, predictable resets, pooled Business quotas, safer treatment of active tasks, and removing or making the 5-hour cap optional when weekly quota remains ( post 570 ). Suggested mitigations include shorter threads/context, cheaper models, disabling unnecessary background features, and carefully testing subagents or concise-output tools, though users stress these do not resolve the underlying pricing and transparency concerns.
