- 2026-08-29T00:34 PaulBellow https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/576 ::  Human-language breakdown of the Codex rate-limits thread Human-language breakdown of the Codex rate-limits thread I went through all 625 available posts , including the context surrounding post 672 . Shortest possible summary: The thread began as a discussion about an expected promotional limit reduction, but evolved into something much larger: users were reporting quota consumption far beyond the expected 2× change, inconsistent behavior between plans and accounts, hidden/background consumption, shifting reset rules, and—most recently—a five-hour cap that prevents people from using most of their remaining weekly allowance when they need it. The argument is no longer simply “the limits are too low.” It is: “Nobody can reliably tell what they bought, what a task will cost, why the meter moved, or whether Codex will be allowed to finish the task it already started.” 1. What actually happened Several separate changes and possible problems became tangled together. Change 1: The promotional 2× allowance ended Codex had been operating under a temporary 2× limits promotion . That promotion ended around early April. A moderator and Community Leader, @VeitB , confirmed that this reduction was real and not merely an interface bug. [1] So, at minimum, users should have expected their effective allowance to be approximately halved. Change 2: Usage moved from message-based to token-sensitive accounting The thread was formally organized around the transition from counting something closer to “messages” toward accounting based on the actual work Codex performs: model used; input, cached-input, and output tokens; repository and context size; reasoning effort; tool calls; local versus cloud operation; compactions; Fast mode; subagents, reviews, automations, and other background work. OpenAI Support later explicitly said: “Codex usage is not based on ‘number of prompts’ or wall-clock time.” [2] That means two prompts can have radically different costs. A tiny visible code change might still require Codex to inspect a large repository, load instructions, run tools and tests, compact context, or perform background operations. Change 3: Business was reduced more than the promotion alone implied VeitB also said that Business limits had been “adjusted even further.” [1:1] That explains why many Business users suddenly found that their more expensive workspace plan appeared to provide dramatically less usable Codex capacity than a personal Plus account. This was not purely imagined or necessarily a universal bug. At least part of the Business reduction was intentional. Possible problem 4: Some reductions were much larger than the announced changes The trouble is that many reports were not describing a simple halving. People reported: several hours of work becoming 10–40 minutes; seven to ten substantial prompts becoming two or three; one simple task consuming 50–75% of a short-term allowance; weekly quotas disappearing after a handful of tasks; quota falling while the user believed Codex was idle. That is where the thread stopped being merely about the promotion ending. Users were observing apparent reductions of 5×, 10×, and occasionally more , which a 2× rollback cannot explain by itself. 2. What does “five-hour limit” really mean? This point caused enormous confusion. A five-hour limit does not mean five hours of Codex work . It means there is a short-term quota bucket associated with a five-hour window. A sufficiently expensive task can empty that bucket in: an hour; 20 minutes; ten minutes; or, according to some reports, one large operation. Similarly, “84% weekly remaining” does not mean a user can necessarily continue working. If the short bucket reaches zero, Codex may stop even though most of the weekly quota remains untouched. That distinction became the central issue in late August. 3. The thread’s main phases Phase A: March — mysterious early display The topic began when Mb briefly saw a UI label reading: “Codex Rate Limit 2 weeks” It disappeared a few hours later and was never conclusively explained. [3] Because topics appear to have been merged, the dates and post numbering are not perfectly chronological. Phase B: April — Business users get hit hard In early April, Business users began reporting extreme consumption. Representative examples included: three coding passes consuming the entire five-hour bucket and 12% of the weekly quota ; [4] one request reportedly consuming 75% of the short-term bucket; [5] three Business seats burning through a credit pool that had previously lasted around a month in approximately two days; [6] work that had previously supported several hours of development lasting only 10–40 minutes. The emotional tone changed quickly from: “Maybe the promotion ended” to: “The product we purchased no longer behaves like the product we evaluated.” That was particularly serious for teams that had bought annual multi-seat Business subscriptions. Phase C: April — OpenAI asks for diagnostics OpenAI Support requested: workspace email; plan; screenshots; timestamps and timezone; HAR files; support case IDs. [7] This created a practical problem: many affected users were working in the CLI or native Codex app, where a normal browser HAR is not available. VeitB then confirmed: the promotion had ended; the Business adjustment was real; unexpected Plus and Pro reductions were being checked; the possible rapid-drain bug had been raised separately. [1:2] That is an important distinction: some reduction was intentional; the most extreme behavior was not clearly confirmed as intentional. Phase D: April — partial recoveries and inconclusive “fixes” Some users later reported that behavior improved after client updates or backend changes. For example, work that had recently exhausted Business quota in 10–15 minutes reportedly lasted 1.5–2 hours again. [8] But other users continued seeing extreme drain at the same time. This indicates the experience was not uniform. Possible explanations include: staged rollouts; plan-specific rate cards; client differences; account configuration; delayed dashboard updates; background activity; genuine bugs affecting only some users. The thread never established one universal April “fix.” Phase E: April–May — new Pro economics Users reported a new $100 Pro 5× tier, temporarily promoted as approximately 10× Plus until May 31 , alongside the $200 Pro 20× tier. [9] Some people upgraded and found it worthwhile. Others interpreted the timing as Plus and Business being degraded to make the new tier attractive. The latter is an understandable inference, but the thread contains no official evidence of that motive . Phase F: May — the clearest official explanation On May 20, OpenAI Support posted the most authoritative explanation in the thread. [2:1] Support said consumption can depend on: model; token use; task and repository size; long threads; large AGENTS.md files; MCP and tool context; compactions; local versus cloud execution; Fast mode; code reviews; automations; subagents; terminal jobs; Memories. This explains why prompt count is a poor measurement. However, it did not answer several major questions: What is the actual capacity of each plan? How are models weighted? How are cached tokens charged? Why did ostensibly identical accounts behave differently? Were the most severe reported reductions intended? Why did resets sometimes move? Phase G: May–July — users begin doing forensic accounting The discussion became much more technically useful here. People examined: local SQLite logs; session JSONL files; cumulative token counters; cached-input ratios; parent and child agent sessions; model-level Analytics; before/after quota percentages. Several valuable discoveries emerged. Automatic review can dominate usage One user found that codex-auto-review accounted for: 523 turns ; 41.12 million tokens ; 74.6% of locally recorded usage . [10] Interactive work represented only 25.4%. That is one of the thread’s strongest findings: sometimes the visible conversation is not the main consumer. An extension can use the wrong billing route Another user discovered that the VS Code extension appeared to be using an OPENAI_API_KEY instead of the expected ChatGPT subscription authentication. Signing out and back in restored the expected behavior. [11] That does not explain general subscription quota depletion, but it is a highly actionable client-specific failure mode. Dashboard and local logs may not attribute activity the same way One user’s Analytics showed many GPT-5.5 turns, while local Codex Desktop session logs showed only GPT-5.4-mini. [12] Possible explanations include: other Codex surfaces being aggregated; reviews or cloud work; hidden/background calls; ordinary ChatGPT activity being classified unexpectedly; local logs not covering all server-side work. The thread never resolved which explanation applied. Do not naïvely add subagent totals Subagents may inherit cumulative parent counters. If you simply add each child’s final reported total, usage can be overstated dramatically. The safer method is to count only the child’s post-fork delta . [13] Phase H: July–August — model differences become more visible A repeated observation was that GPT-5.5 consumed quota substantially faster than GPT-5.4 for some users. Switching back to 5.4 sometimes made Codex usable again. [14] This is consistent with OpenAI Support’s statement that GPT-5.5 was more expensive under the relevant rate card. Later, the discussion shifted to GPT-5.6 models: Sol: strongest but often very expensive; Terra: intended as a more economical middle option; Luna: cheaper fallback, but with weaker performance or missing tools in some reports; Luna Reserve: a separate emergency allowance shown to some users after the main short-term bucket was exhausted. The crucial practical lesson was that a nominally cheaper model is not always cheaper per successful task . If Luna or Terra needs several correction passes and Sol succeeds once, the “cheaper” model may consume more total quota. Phase I: August — evidence of a possible real capacity decline The strongest community attempt at controlled measurement came from ANS1337. They normalized local logs across several historical Pro 5× weekly windows. Earlier windows clustered around approximately 13.5K–15K normalized workload units . The newest window reached 100% at 9,613 . That implied roughly: a 33% decline from the historical median; or a 28.9% decline from the immediately preceding comparable window. [15] This is meaningful evidence that the account’s observed capacity changed. It does not , by itself, prove why: the nominal allowance may have been reduced; model weights may have changed; hidden background work may be missing from local logs; account routing may have changed; the meter may have malfunctioned. But it is much stronger than “it felt faster.” Phase J: Late August — the five-hour cap returns This became the thread’s dominant and most current issue. A quoted announcement attributed to Tibo described restoring the five-hour cap for Plus while keeping it disabled temporarily for the $100 and $200 Pro tiers. [16] Team and Business users then reported that they had also received the cap, even though those plans were apparently not named in the quoted announcement. Users repeatedly found themselves in situations such as: five-hour quota: 0% ; weekly quota: 80–90% remaining . Representative reports included: a Plus user exhausting the short allowance in 1 hour 15 minutes while retaining 84% weekly ; [17] Sol High using 52% of the short allowance in 23 minutes 45 seconds ; [18] another user exhausting the short bucket in about 51 minutes while retaining 84% weekly . [19] Sol XHigh allegedly exhausting it in around ten minutes. The most serious reported change was behavioral: users said Codex did not always finish or safely checkpoint the active operation. It could simply hit zero and stop mid-task. That creates additional cost because the next session must: reconstruct context; inspect half-completed work; determine what succeeded; rerun checks; repair interrupted changes. The cap can therefore generate the very consumption it is supposed to constrain. 4. What appears to be genuinely established These conclusions have comparatively strong support. Confirmed or strongly supported The 2× promotion ended. Business received an additional adjustment. Codex accounting is not based simply on prompt count or elapsed time. Model, context, repository size, tools, compaction, execution surface, and background work affect consumption. Fast mode costs more quota. Memories, reviews, automations and subagents can consume usage in the background. GPT-5.5 could be more expensive than GPT-5.4 under the relevant rate card. Some users experienced client, authentication, attribution, or background-work problems. By late August, Plus and reportedly Team/Business accounts had a short-term cap alongside the weekly allowance. The short-term cap could prevent continued work while most of the weekly quota remained. The user experience varied substantially across accounts and periods. The thread contains no direct late-August OpenAI explanation of the current behavior. 5. What is still unknown Despite hundreds of posts, the thread does not establish: the absolute five-hour allowance for each tier; the absolute weekly allowance for each tier; the current per-model weighting; exactly how cached input affects subscription quota; whether purchased credits use the same formula as included quota; whether Business is a fixed percentage below Plus; whether accounts on the same plan can intentionally receive different capacities; why resets sometimes re-anchor the weekly window; why bonus resets can apparently disadvantage users who still had quota; whether normal ChatGPT Web activity shares the Codex meter; why Team/Business apparently received the restored short-term cap; whether Pro 5× and 20× remain exempt everywhere; whether tasks are intended to receive a graceful completion period; whether the major June and August changes were policy, model weighting, hidden usage, or bugs. That uncertainty is itself one of the thread’s primary findings. 6. Why apparently tiny prompts can be expensive The user may type: “Rename this field.” But Codex may still: load system and repository instructions; load AGENTS.md ; search the repository; inspect references; read multiple files; run MCP tools; update or consult Memories; reason through compatibility; modify code; run tests; inspect failures; retry; compact context; produce the final response. So a one-line diff is not necessarily a one-line operation. That explains some surprising costs—but not necessarily all of the extreme cases. Reports involving empty projects, idle periods, mismatched dashboards, or controlled repeat tests remain difficult to explain solely through repository size. 7. The most useful practical advice from the thread Check background consumption first Inspect: automatic code review; GitHub review integration; cloud tasks; automations; subagents; background terminal jobs; Memories; unfinished browser or Work tasks. Given the 74.6% auto-review example, this should happen before obsessively shortening individual prompts. Verify the authentication and billing identity Check whether the client is using: ChatGPT subscription authentication; an OPENAI_API_KEY ; workspace credits; personal API billing. After extension or desktop updates, signing out and back in may be worthwhile. Collect evidence before reporting the problem For one reproducible run, record: plan; model; reasoning level; Fast/Ultra setting; local or cloud; client and version; timestamp and timezone; session ID; short and weekly percentages before and after; local token deltas; background features enabled. A support report with this bundle is much more useful than “Codex used 40% for one prompt.” Use controlled comparisons Test the same bounded task with: a fresh thread; the same repository state; one model changed at a time; no background reviews or automations; no Fast mode unless specifically testing it. That helps separate model cost from accumulated context. Keep projects resumable Maintain a concise checkpoint containing: current objective; completed work; remaining work; architecture decisions; known failures; commands and tests; changed files. Combine that with small Git commits or worktrees. This does not fix rate limits, but it dramatically reduces the damage caused by interruptions. Optimize for completed work, not cheap tokens Do not automatically assume the smallest model is the best deal. Measure: quota consumed per correct, tested, completed task rather than: quota consumed per turn. Stop no-progress loops One reported operation continued planning, compacting and retrying for hours without completing. A useful instruction is: If there is no material progress after N attempts, stop, summarize the blocker, and ask for guidance. Be careful with banked or bonus resets Users reported that activating a reset could restart the seven-day clock rather than adding quota to the existing schedule. If that behavior applies, redeeming a reset while substantial weekly quota remains can waste value. This was repeatedly observed but not officially documented. 8. What users are actually asking OpenAI to change The best proposals were not demands for unlimited Sol. They were requests for understandable and controllable limits. Transparent accounting Users want to see, per task: model; input; cached input; output; tool use; background use; quota deducted; applicable multiplier; which bucket was charged. Absolute plan capacities Percentages are nearly meaningless if nobody knows what 100% represents or whether 100% changed from last week. Stable reset semantics A reset should clearly state whether it: tops up the existing period; replaces the existing period; starts a new seven-day window; affects reserve pools; changes the next scheduled reset. Graceful task completion A strong recurring proposal was: prevent new turns when the short limit is reached; allow the current turn to finish or checkpoint safely; save resumable task state automatically. Optional or configurable short-term protection Instead of an inflexible cap: let users opt in or out; let users set a percentage of weekly quota available per burst; offer a burst mode; use a soft warning; allow Pro/Business administrators to configure it. The clearest expression was: “I don’t want more usage. I want control over the usage I already have.” Post 572 9. The strongest arguments on both sides The case for the limits Top-model agentic coding is expensive. A visible “small change” may involve enormous invisible context. Subscription inference is probably subsidized relative to intensive API use. Short-term limits can protect platform reliability. Business pricing includes governance, privacy and administration—not only tokens. A $20 subscription cannot reasonably provide unlimited autonomous Sol development. These are fair points. The case against the implementation Users cannot forecast task cost. Effective capacities appear to change without adequate notice. Percentages hide the actual purchased quantity. Business customers committed annually before the behavior changed. Active work can be interrupted despite abundant weekly quota. Purchased credits lack satisfactory task-level reconciliation. Bonus resets may silently move the reset date. Support did not publicly close the loop on major investigations. Users expend extra quota reconstructing interrupted work. The strongest criticism is therefore not: “Compute should be free.” It is: “Paid compute should be measurable, predictable, attributable, and safely interruptible.” 10. Assessment of your linked post 672 The linked post is an AI-generated summary attributed to GPT-5.6 Sol . [20] What it gets right It accurately captures: the promotion ending; the token-accounting transition; Business being adjusted more heavily; the unexpectedly rapid drain reports; context and tool-related explanations; the failed multi-account-penalty theory; automatic review as hidden consumption; accidental API-key routing; OpenAI Support’s May diagnostic advice; transparency and graceful-stop requests. As a compact summary of the April–July thread, it is good. What it underplays It does not adequately represent the newest and most important late-August development: the restored five-hour cap; Team/Business apparently receiving it without being named; users having 80–90% weekly allowance but being unable to work; active tasks reportedly being stopped mid-operation; Sol/Terra/Luna and Luna Reserve differences; banked and global resets re-anchoring the week; the Pro 5× forensic evidence of a possible 29–33% capacity decline; cross-surface reset and capability inconsistencies. It also says Plus/Pro reductions were being investigated without emphasizing that, months later, the thread still had no posted resolution. Finally, its placement matters. Immediately before it, users were arguing over whether elaborate MCP, worktree, subagent, and scheduled-continuation systems should be required just to cope with the quota. One reply summarized that problem well: “At some point you’re spending more time engineering around the quota system than doing the actual work you opened Codex for.” Post 671 Post 672 generalized the thread, but lost some of that immediate practical force. The gold from them thar hills If I had to reduce the entire thread to ten useful lessons: The visible prompt is often not the main cost. Check reviews, Memories, tools, automations and subagents. Verify which account and billing system the client is actually using. Never interpret “five-hour allowance” as five hours of work. Do not add cumulative subagent counters naïvely. A cheaper model can be more expensive per successful task. Fresh threads reduce accumulated context, but create state-reconstruction costs—checkpoint first. Stop unattended no-progress loops before they consume an entire allowance. Treat resets carefully; they may re-anchor rather than simply add quota. Support needs forensic before/after evidence, not just prompt counts. The deepest problem is not merely quota size—it is the absence of a stable, auditable contract for what the quota represents. Final verdict The thread does not prove one universal Codex metering bug. It shows a combination of: intentional promotional rollback; intentional Business reductions; token- and model-sensitive accounting; hidden/background work; client and authentication problems; likely staged or inconsistent rollouts; unexplained account-level discrepancies; possible capacity or weighting changes; and a late-August short-term cap whose design conflicts badly with burst-oriented development. The most defensible human-language conclusion is: OpenAI has valid reasons to limit expensive agentic coding, but users currently lack enough visibility and control to distinguish an expensive task, a background consumer, a plan change, a reset quirk, and an actual metering problem. That is why the thread remained angry for hundreds of posts: not simply because Codex costs money, but because the meter often behaves like a black box while controlling whether paid work may continue. I need an itemized usage record. Metered usage should come with a receipt. I should also be able to audit previous tasks. Right now, if I discover that I consumed a huge portion of my allowance yesterday, I should be able to go backward and answer: What did I ask ChatGPT/Codex to do? What model did it use? What apps/tools/plugins did it invoke? How many files did it process? How much usage did that task consume? Why was one task substantially more expensive than another? That is not just about accountability. It would teach me how to use the product more efficiently. If I discover that one type of request routinely consumes 200 credits while another consumes 10, I can change how I work. At the moment, I cannot learn

- 2026-09-05T14:41 azamboni https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/650 ::  The previous discussion about this behavior has already been closed, so I am opening a new topic because I think there is an additional issue that has not been fully addressed. This is not primarily a bug report. I understand that the current behavior is intentional. My concern is that the term “Full Reset” does not accurately describe what the user is actually receiving. What happened to me My Codex weekly allowance was scheduled to reset on: Sunday, September 6 at 11:30 PM I exhausted my allowance on: Saturday, September 5 So there were only about 37 hours remaining until my normal weekly reset. I had a banked Full Reset , and I redeemed it because I had a lot of work to complete. My reasonable interpretation was: Current allowance: 0% → Full Reset → 100% while my already scheduled Sunday reset would remain unchanged. Instead, after redeeming it: Current allowance: 0% → 100% but Next reset: Sunday, September 6 → approximately seven days after redemption So the previous reset date disappeared. That is not economically equivalent to a reset What actually happened was effectively: I advanced by approximately 37 hours the weekly allowance that I was already going to receive on Sunday. That is much closer to: “Advance your next weekly allowance” or “Start your next weekly usage period now.” It is materially different from what most users will understand by the word reset . The distinction becomes particularly important near the end of a weekly cycle. If somebody redeems a Full Reset with six days remaining until their normal reset, they receive nearly six days of accelerated access. If somebody redeems exactly the same Full Reset with only 37 hours remaining, as I did, they receive only approximately 37 hours of acceleration. Yet the UI presents both transactions as the same Full Reset . The previous discussion addressed disclosure There was already a community discussion about the fact that redeeming a banked reset moves the weekly reset date. OpenAI confirmed that this behavior is intentional and agreed that the consequence should be clearer before confirmation. I think there is a second issue: the terminology itself is misleading. Even with better disclosure, calling this operation a Full Reset suggests that something additional is being reset. In practice, the product is bringing the user’s next normal weekly entitlement forward and re-anchoring the seven-day cycle. Suggested UX Before redemption, the confirmation screen should show something like: Current scheduled reset: September 6, 11:30 PM If you redeem this now: Usage returns to 100% immediately. Your existing September 6 reset will no longer occur. Your new weekly reset will be: approximately September 12, [time] And ideally the feature should be renamed to something such as: Start next weekly allowance now or Advance weekly renewal rather than Full Reset . This would allow users to understand the actual value of the decision before redeeming it. Had I been shown explicitly that I was exchanging my banked reset essentially for only ~37 hours of earlier access, I would almost certainly have waited until Sunday. The system may be working as designed. My point is that the current name and confirmation flow do not accurately communicate the transaction being made by the user.

It helped mount the NAS share read only and used ffprobe to inventory the collection, around 654 files and 775 GB. To be clear, it did not transcode 775 GB. It read metadata only, and the NAS stayed read only the whole time. The result was useful. Almost everything should Direct Play, a handful of old AVI, Xvid and DivX files need practical testing, and two files need special handling. Then it prepared the build directory. In other words, discovery was done and we were ready to actually start building. Then I checked the meter: 23% remaining. Roughly two hours, around 77% of my weekly allowance, and the actual app did not exist yet. No APK, no backend, no library, no metadata, no UI and no deployment. Just technical discovery and preparation. I understand that agentic development costs compute, and I am not asking for unlimited usage on Plus. But a “weekly” allowance that loses three quarters of its capacity during two hours of preparation does not feel like a weekly development budget. It feels like a few hours of development spread across an entire week. What am I supposed to do with the remaining 23%? Start building the APK and hope the meter survives? Stop debugging halfway through and wait for the next reset? The worst part is the unpredictability. I had no way of knowing beforehand that this work would consume 77% of the weekly allowance. So, concretely, what would actually help: Show per task consumption and break it down. Repository context, reasoning, tool calls or whatever is actually consuming the allowance. Warn me before an expensive task is likely to consume a large share of the remaining weekly allowance, so I can decide whether I want to run it. Include enough predictable capacity to finish at least one meaningful development milestone in a single working session. The capability of Codex is not the problem. It is genuinely capable and careful with my systems, which is exactly why this is so frustrating. I want to use it. But the limits are unpredictable enough that I cannot trust Codex to carry a project from start to finish. And this is what happens in practice when the meter runs out: I hand the unfinished project over to Claude and continue working there. I have worked through long development sessions with Claude and personally have never run into a comparable situation where I suddenly had to stop working because the available usage was gone. With Codex, this keeps happening to me after relatively short periods of serious work. That is the part I do not understand from a product perspective. I am paying for ChatGPT Plus. I want to use Codex. Codex is capable of doing the work. But the usage limits repeatedly push me toward a competing product simply because I can continue working there. I’m not asking for infinite compute. I’m asking for predictable limits and enough usable capacity to finish a meaningful piece of work.

- 2026-09-08T18:01 robert-lukowski https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/690 ::  I’ve been seeing the same issue and initially focused on how quickly Astra was consuming the available budget. After looking at my own workflow, though, I realized that part of the problem was resource allocation. I was effectively using the strongest reasoning model for repository exploration, routine implementation, tests and other tasks that could be handled elsewhere. I’m now experimenting with routing different parts of an engineering task to different agents/models: Router → Context Scout → Premium Reasoning → Implementer → Reviewer The goal is to minimize the repository context reaching the premium model and reserve it for decisions that actually require deep reasoning. It doesn’t change Astra’s underlying usage limits, but it may make the available reasoning budget much more useful. I’ve published the experiment as an open-source GitHub template called Token Gourmand under my GitHub profile robert-lukowski. It’s still early, and I’d be especially interested in comparing real measurements from different workflows rather than making assumptions about the savings.

- 2026-09-12T19:23 KrishnaV https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/743 ::  I think you’re still arguing against a claim I never made. I have never said AI makes a beginner equal to a senior developer. In my original message I explicitly said that developers have a major advantage because they understand the code, know what to look for, and can catch problems instead of blindly trusting the AI. I also later said, word for word, that AI “doesn’t make an inexperienced person equal to a senior developer.” My point is that AI has changed the path from being inexperienced to being capable of building real software. Before AI, someone with a strong idea but very little programming experience had a massive gap between the idea and actually creating it. They either had to spend years learning first, find someone who could build it, or have enough money to hire developers. Now that person can start building while they are learning. They can ask questions, generate an implementation, test it, see what fails, investigate why it failed, discover concepts they did not know before, improve the design, and repeat that process. That is why I don’t think the 0.1 × 100 versus 20 × 100 example works. That model assumes AI is just a fixed multiplier applied equally to everyone’s existing ability. But that is not what is happening. For an experienced developer, AI might make them faster, automate repetitive work, help them explore solutions, or make debugging easier. For a beginner, AI can unlock entire categories of work that were previously outside their usable capability. Going from “I have no idea how to implement this” to “I can build a working version with assistance, test it, inspect the failures, understand more of what went wrong, and iterate” is not just multiplying the beginner’s old capability by some arbitrary constant. And yes, an experienced developer will still know about problems the beginner might not recognize: architecture, security, race conditions, scaling, performance, failure modes, and plenty of other things. But beginners are not necessarily sitting there believing they already understand everything. A reasonable beginner knows there are concepts outside their current knowledge. AI can actually help expose those gaps earlier by pointing out problems, explaining unfamiliar concepts, suggesting tests, or telling the user that an assumption they made is wrong. That does not magically give someone twenty years of engineering judgment. It changes how quickly they can reach new knowledge and how early they can start applying it. That is also why I disagree with your “1 in a million” framing for someone starting with little money or experience, especially when you said yourself that the number was an assumption. You are looking heavily at where someone starts. I am looking at what happens to that person over time. A founder can fail, learn why they failed, try again, gain experience, build connections, improve their judgment, and make another attempt. The person making attempt five is not the same person who made attempt one. That is where grit matters. I do not mean blindly repeating the same bad idea forever. I mean being willing to keep learning, adapting, and trying instead of treating the first failure as proof that you should give up and go do something else. AI makes that process even more significant because it reduces the cost and time of iteration. Someone can make more attempts, encounter harder problems sooner, learn from those problems, and build experience while actually creating things. So again, my claim is not that beginners suddenly catch up to senior developers. My claim is that AI has substantially lowered the barrier between having an idea and being able to build, test, learn, iterate, and eventually become capable. Experienced developers still have a huge advantage. What has changed is that “become an experienced developer first, then start building serious things” is no longer the only realistic path.

- 2026-09-13T19:44 logremonte https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/754 ::  Today I got a very clear example of how the current usage-limit system can completely derail real project work. I was working on a project that, under normal uninterrupted conditions, should have taken no more than about 3 hours. Because of the 5-hour limit, waiting for resets, interruptions, and constantly having to return to the project context, it ended up costing me more than 12 hours. And this is the core issue: the 5-hour limit, in its current form, makes Codex unsuitable for serious, continuous project work. I can understand having a weekly limit to some extent — users can at least manage their usage around it. But an additional 5-hour limit creates an absurd situation where you can still have plenty of weekly usage available, yet after a few hours of normal work the tool simply stops. Even worse, it can stop in the middle of an operation that has already started . In my case, during the final round of changes on GPT-6 Astra, Very High , the model started the task, modified project files, began running the next commands, and then hit the usage limit. It did not finish the build, the tests, or the verification. The project was left in an intermediate state, and afterwards I had to determine again what had actually been completed and what had been interrupted. This is not just a minor inconvenience or a matter of “wait two hours and come back.” In software development, this kind of behavior means: loss of workflow continuity, additional debugging, repeated testing, the risk of leaving a project in an inconsistent state, and, most importantly, a very real loss of the user’s time. If the system knows that a usage limit is about to be reached, it should not allow the agent to begin an operation it will not be able to finish. Alternatively, it should guarantee the atomic completion of an operation that has already started , and only block further work afterwards. But an even better solution would be to remove the 5-hour limit entirely, or fundamentally redesign it . If OpenAI wants Codex to be treated as a serious tool for real software-development work, continuity and predictability cannot be optional. For projects that require several hours of focused work, they are basic requirements. In my case: a task that should have taken around 3 hours → more than 12 hours of work, and at the finish line the agent was stopped in the middle of the final changes because of the 5-hour limit. That is the exact opposite of what you expect from a tool that is supposed to save time. OpenAI really needs to rethink this mechanism. - 2026-09-17T12:39 Numa https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/783 ::  This is not about banked resets. It is about automatic hard resets triggered by OpenAI. A banked reset is useful because I can decide when to redeem it. An automatic hard reset is fundamentally different—especially when it also changes the date of the next regular weekly reset. Here is a simple example: My regular weekly reset is scheduled for Saturday. I start the week with 100% and carefully budget that allowance across my workdays. By Tuesday, I have used 40%, leaving me with 60% until Saturday. On Wednesday, you unexpectedly reset my allowance to 100% and move the next reset to the following Wednesday. At first glance, this looks as though I have received additional capacity. For professional planning, however, it is not a reliably usable bonus. Before the intervention, I still had 60% available until Saturday and expected to receive a fresh weekly allowance on Saturday. After the intervention, I have 100%, but that allowance may now have to last until the following Wednesday. The scheduled Saturday reset has disappeared. That leaves me with two bad options: I assume that another unexpected reset will occur, use my allowance more quickly, and risk running out of capacity in the middle of an important project if it does not happen. I assume that no further reset will occur and carefully spread the new 100% across the next seven days. Only the second option is professionally responsible. But it also means that the automatic reset has not created any additional predictable capacity. It is genuinely helpful only if I was already close to zero. This creates the wrong incentive: users who consume their allowance quickly and speculate on another reset benefit the most. Users who plan responsibly cannot safely make use of the supposed gift. There is also a second problem. Over the past several months, these automatic resets occurred frequently enough—sometimes once, twice, or even three times within a single week—that many intensive users adapted their real-world workflows to the capacity that was consistently available in practice. That is exactly what happened in our case. We organized our ongoing development and administrative workflows around the capacity that had been available in practice for months. During the past two weeks, this established practice suddenly changed significantly, without clear communication. This has not merely affected our workflows in theory. Several ongoing tasks have had to be interrupted, postponed, or continued much more cautiously than planned. Important development work is now sometimes left untouched for days because we need to reserve the remaining allowance for urgent tasks. We pay for the Pro plan precisely because we use Codex seriously and regularly. Yet we can currently no longer plan with confidence whether a project we start can be completed within the same working week. We would prefer to continue using OpenAI and Codex. However, if the available capacity remains this unpredictable, we will have to seriously consider moving a larger share of our work to other providers or self-hosted models. This is not a threat. It is a necessary response to the fact that professional workflows require reliable operating conditions. I am not claiming that promotional resets must continue forever, nor am I claiming any entitlement to unlimited additional capacity. What I am asking for is clear communication and a system that allows professional users to plan their work reliably. Automatically granted additional capacity should therefore take one of the following forms: Bonus capacity that does not change the existing weekly reset date; A banked reset that users can redeem when they actually need it; Preservation of the unused remainder of the existing allowance; Or a clearly published and reliable reset schedule. During a fundamental transition like this, providing a few additional banked resets to affected Pro users would also be a fair temporary measure. It would allow us to complete work that is already underway while adapting our workflows to the changed capacity. An automatic reset that replaces the remaining allowance and simultaneously postpones the next regular reset is not a reliable benefit. It turns capacity planning into a gamble. Please do not confuse this issue with user-controlled banked resets. Banked resets are useful precisely because users can decide when to redeem them. The actual problem is the unpredictable hard reset triggered by OpenAI—and the sudden change to a practice that had been established for months.

The purpose of the smoke test was specifically to protect me from defective Codex implementation, defective provider integration, and wasted production runs. Instead, over approximately one hour, Codex turned that smoke test into an enormous sequence of speculative implementation changes, repeated approval interruptions, failed disposable patches, provider calls that should not have been necessary, and repeated rediscovery of defects that the smoke test itself was supposed to detect safely and cheaply. During this process I exhausted my five-hour usage allowance and, within the same day, consumed approximately half of my weekly allowance. This is no longer functioning as a productivity tool. The tool intended to protect me from implementation failure became the principal source of implementation failure and resource consumption. I want this feedback to record EVERY material failure and EVERY repeated NO / prohibition that had to be issued during this incident. ================================================== ORIGINAL FAILURES THAT CAUSED THE SMOKE TEST 1. Week-Day authoring failed because generated hashtags violated the declared compact contract. The contract required: · 3–12 hashtags · unique values · strings · every hashtag matching ^#[A-Za-z0-9]+$ The provider returned several values without the required # prefix. The deterministic validator correctly rejected the response. 2. Investigation established that the actual compact response contract survived correctly into the authored request. Therefore the original compact contract itself was not missing. 3. Codex then established the FIRST transport defect: compact_provider_dispatch.py discarded the real compact response contract and replaced the response schema passed downstream with: {“required”:[]} 4. Codex established the SECOND transport defect: the Codex-native adapter received response_schema but did not forward it into: author_json(…, output_schema=…) The actual author_json kwargs contained only the timeout. So the system had a declared compact response contract which was included in prompt text but was not mechanically enforced through the structured-output interface. 5. Separately, the second Week-Day run failed because the word: proof in perfectly legitimate prose: “One clue is not proof. Several together are a reason to stay out.” was rejected as: TAXONOMY_LEAK This exposed an unrelated overbroad taxonomy validator. The original purpose of that taxonomy guard was to prevent actual internal taxonomy identifiers from appearing visibly in creative output. Instead it globally banned ordinary English words such as “proof”. That second defect was explicitly separated from the hashtag transport smoke so that Codex would not drift into redesigning unrelated validation. ================================================== FAILURE 1 — CODEX BUILT THE WRONG FIRST SMOKE HARNESS Codex initially built a hashtag transport smoke test containing a manually reconstructed synthetic schema: schema = { … “pattern”: “^#[A-Za-z0-9]+$” … } This was rejected. NO. The test was required to use the ACTUAL captured Week-Day compact response contract. The whole point was to prove: actual contract → dispatcher → adapter → author_json(output_schema) Instead Codex initially created a substitute schema inside the smoke harness. That could have produced a false success because it would prove that a hand-created test schema could be transported, not that the actual production contract was transported. Codex had to be explicitly told: NO reconstructed schema. NO approximation. NO manually duplicated hashtag rule. Use the actual Week-Day contract artifact. ================================================== FAILURE 2 — THE FIRST CORRECT BASELINE EXPOSED TWO SHARED TRANSPORT FAILURES After correction, the provider-free transport smoke finally showed: source contract: intact embedded contract == source contract: TRUE dispatcher output: {“required”:[]} dispatcher schema == source contract: FALSE author_json output_schema: absent / null provider_calls: 0 This was useful. This was what a smoke test was supposed to do. It isolated the shared transport defects without a provider call. At this point Codex should have proceeded in a disciplined manner. Instead the implementation loop exploded. ================================================== FAILURE 3 — CODEX DISCARDED ITS OWN LOCAL-CONSTRAINT INVENTORY Codex created a converter returning: response_schema, locally_enforced and then did: response_schema, _locally_enforced = … The inventory was calculated and immediately discarded. NO. Codex had specifically been instructed that unsupported compact constraints must never silently disappear. Every constraint had to be accounted for as either: · represented in provider schema · deterministically enforced locally · semantic/prompt-only · unresolved Instead, the first implementation threw the evidence away. ================================================== FAILURE 4 — CODEX ADDED AN UNPROVEN $schema DECLARATION Codex inserted: “$schema”: “http:// json-schema . org / draft / 2020-12 / schema” without having established that Codex author_json(output_schema=…) accepted that keyword or dialect declaration. NO. The smoke was intended to minimize unknown variables. Codex introduced another unknown provider-schema keyword before establishing support for it. It had to be explicitly told: NO unproven $schema keyword. ================================================== FAILURE 5 — CODEX CREATED AN UNREACHABLE units BRANCH The converter contained logic equivalent to: if isinstance(descriptor, dict): … elif field == “units”: … Therefore the special unit-count handling would never execute when the units descriptor was a dict. NO. There was also significant duplicated work: both children inherited the full parent context ( fork_turns="all" ), and the parent continued repository investigation while the children investigated overlapping schema/grants/RLS/bootstrap areas. I later changed service_tier to default , fixed the orchestration/context rules, synchronized the repo, restarted the apps, and performed one controlled rerun of the same Supabase assessment . I realize that is no longer a one-variable experiment, so I wouldn’t attribute the improvement solely to Fast mode, but the result may still be useful: Sol High architect Fast/Priority off no investigation subagents task completed 37 model continuations ~ 4.01M cumulative input ~ 3.85M cached input ~ 19K output maximum/final request context ~ 166K 5-hour usage: approximately 0% → 16% weekly usage increased by approximately 3 percentage points So the original 0→100% event appears to be a real combination of very high cumulative agentic traffic, full-context duplication across concurrent agents, and Priority/Fast being enabled—not simply 2m23s of wall-clock work. I also have the raw JSONL telemetry, but I don’t think it is appropriate to post publicly because it contains local paths, repository contents/structure, prompts, commands/tool outputs and other potentially sensitive information. I can share a sanitized per-request telemetry extract if that would be useful. I also have the exact ChatGPT/Codex desktop build and can add the session/Feedback ID and later allowance snapshots if those are useful for correlating this with server-side accounting.

- 2026-09-30T07:28 jochenschultz https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/861 ::  Well, what I found so far is the following: GPT 6.1-sol It just does what you tell it to do - although you have to keep an eye on the implementation of LLM - because: the models in general tend to use too many tokens. Let me give an example. I have build a logo generator as part of my SaaS builder system and I wanted it to get me a text logo with an icon. First it implemented a flow where it first made the logo, then used that as an example for a second llm call to generate the icon that I want when a sidebar is minimized. As you can tell the icon wasn’t really good. So I asked it to prompt the model to use a specified area for the icon and the text beside that. Which lead to a control flow where the model decided to check if the icon overlaps the area. Which then went into a loop because it happened pretty much every time. I solved it by explaining the model that I want it to OCR the text and remove it and then cut out the icon. Which is not only faster than a new image generation but also more reliable. And then I told it to write a script that inverts the colors so I got 4 images (two text + icon and two icons for darkmode and lightmode). I havn’t tried by telling it to do it as determnistic as possible and it is only allowed to use one llm call. But at this point I doubt it would have solved it without serious api cost. But then on the other hand when I asked it to build some modules it was even following i18n and theme compatibelity without me mentioning it. And the other models never did that, not weven astra did that reliable. And anotehr thing that I found is: I had a couple files opened - huge files - and i didn’t remove the option where the model should use opened files for the context. - 2026-10-04T08:53 Baron_Whiskey https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/872 ::  I think I may have reverse-engineered the relationship between the Codex/Work 5 hour time-slot allowance and the weekly allowance. My hypothesis is that one complete time-slot allowance is exactly: 5/32 of the weekly allowance = 15.625% The interesting part is that I did NOT start with 5/32 and try to make the observations fit it. I had screenshots from actual usage showing the weekly allowance after one fully consumed time slot, after two fully consumed time slots, and during a partially consumed time slot. Because the UI reports the weekly allowance as a whole-number percentage, I treated those displayed percentages as rounded measurements rather than exact values. Comparing the observations let me narrow the apparent per-slot consumption to approximately: 15.5%–15.75% of the weekly allowance Only afterward did I notice that sitting almost exactly in the middle of that independently derived interval is: 15.625% And: 15.625% = 5/32 = 5/2⁵ That caught my attention because 32 is a power of two, making it a very natural denominator in software, while 5 is a very human-sized allocation. There is also a funny symmetry to it: the numerator is 5 and the denominator is 2 raised to the 5th power. So my working hypothesis is: Weekly allowance = 32 allocation units One full short-term window = 5 allocation units That would explain why people commonly observe a complete time slot consuming “about 16%” of their weekly allowance. The actual value would be 15.625%, which a whole-number UI would naturally make look like roughly 16%. To be clear: I am NOT claiming that I have access to OpenAI’s internal implementation or that 5/32 is confirmed. This is an empirical hypothesis inferred from the displayed quota behavior. What makes me think it is worth testing is the order in which I found it: observed usage → rounding constraints → 15.5–15.75% → noticed 15.625% → realized 15.625% = 5/32 So, OpenAI engineers: did I find the number? And if it really is 5/2⁵, was the symmetry intentional? Either way, I would be interested to see whether other people’s usage data fits 15.625% more closely than a nominal 15% or 16%.

- 2026-10-08T14:13 hortrame https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/887 ::  Your “5x or 20x of what?” question captures the product-accounting problem cleanly. Even if a disputed event turns out to be ordinary charging, a multiplier over an opaque, behavior-dependent base is difficult to budget or audit. Structured incident records: github.com/hortrame-systems/openai_dispute Community coordination: Codex/Pro usage transparency and incident index opened 01:36PM - 08 Oct 26 UTC hortrame-systems # Purpose This issue is a public coordination space for users reporting abrupt … changes in Codex/Work usage, Pro value, five-hour or weekly allowance depletion, paid-credit consumption, tier behavior, repeated work, reset behavior, or related accounting/transparency problems. It is **not** a declaration that every report shares one cause. Some cases may be explained by concurrent subagents, duplicated context, Priority/Fast service tier, model choice, ordinary charging, or other factors. Those explanations belong here too. The goal is to turn scattered anecdotes into comparable evidence without asking people to spend more money reproducing failures. ## What we are trying to answer 1. **What did the customer buy?** 2. **What actually consumed the allowance or credits?** 3. **Can the customer independently reconcile the two?** 4. **Which cases share a mechanism, and which do not?** 5. **What provider-side records are needed when the client-side record is insufficient?** ## Do not spend money to participate Please do **not** buy extra credits or deliberately rerun an expensive failure just to contribute here. Natural incidents, existing screenshots, session metadata, logs, support replies, and controlled tests you were already going to perform are enough. ## Zero-touch agent collection If you do not want to fill the incident template manually, give your local AI agent the automation instructions here: https://github.com/hortrame-systems/openai_dispute/blob/main/AGENT_DATA_COLLECTION.md The collector is designed to run passively from existing local/session evidence. It must not buy credits, rerun failures, alter settings, inspect unrelated data, or publish raw/private material. It can automatically normalize evidence, detect candidate incidents, generate sanitized incident reports, and optionally submit them to this issue after the operator explicitly enables automatic submission. ## Minimal incident record Copy this block into a comment and fill only what you actually know. ```text INCIDENT ID: DATE/TIME: TIMEZONE: PLAN: SURFACE: Codex / Work / Desktop / other APP/CLIENT VERSION: MODEL: REASONING LEVEL: SERVICE TIER / FAST / PRIORITY STATE: known / unknown PARENT AGENT COUNT: SUBAGENT COUNT: TASK DESCRIPTION (sanitized): TASK DURATION: 5-HOUR ALLOWANCE BEFORE: 5-HOUR ALLOWANCE AFTER: WEEKLY ALLOWANCE BEFORE: WEEKLY ALLOWANCE AFTER: MONTHLY/RESERVE BEFORE: MONTHLY/RESERVE AFTER: PAID CREDIT BALANCE BEFORE: PAID CREDIT BALANCE AFTER: RESET OR RELOAD DURING WINDOW: KNOWN CONCURRENT WORK: KNOWN CONTEXT/COMPACTION EVENTS: SESSION / THREAD / FEEDBACK ID (sanitized if needed): SUPPORT CASE: RELATED GITHUB ISSUE: OBSERVED FACTS: INFERENCES / HYPOTHESES: ALTERNATIVE EXPLANATIONS: WHAT WOULD FALSIFY YOUR CURRENT HYPOTHESIS: EVIDENCE LINKS: CORRECTIONS: ``` Unknown fields should stay **unknown**. Do not estimate them just to complete the form. ## High-value evidence Especially useful: - before/after allowance or credit screenshots; - exact local timestamps + timezone; - task/session IDs; - app/client version; - model and reasoning level; - whether subagents were spawned; - whether Priority/Fast was enabled; - sanitized JSONL/log excerpts showing usage or parent/child relationships; - support replies; - evidence that a dramatic case was actually explained by ordinary charging or configuration. Negative evidence is evidence. ## Privacy Do not post: - credentials or tokens; - private prompts; - client/customer material; - payment-card data; - raw account identifiers; - secrets or internal business data. Sanitize first. Preserve originals privately. ## Separate questions that must not be collapsed At least four mechanisms may produce superficially similar complaints: 1. **Product-value change:** the same subscription provides less predictable or less useful capacity. 2. **Client/runtime behavior:** repeated work, state loss, unexpected subagents, duplicated context, compaction, etc. 3. **Tier/pricing behavior:** Priority/Fast selection, model changes, effective credit rates. 4. **Accounting/reconciliation:** debits or allowance movement that cannot be reconstructed from customer-visible records. One incident may involve more than one mechanism. Do not assume it does. ## Shared provider-side request Where client evidence is insufficient, the useful provider-side fields are: - historical tier/configuration state; - authorization provenance; - request parentage; - request-level usage; - account/funding-pool attribution; - effective pricing/tier resolution; - debit and balance transitions; - settlement/reconciliation records. Opaque IDs or provider attestations are acceptable where direct disclosure is inappropriate. ## Current released reference package HORTRAME's minimized public forensic package is one case study, not the master explanation for everyone: - Repository: https://github.com/hortrame-systems/openai_dispute - Zenodo DOI: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23192953 - Public archive SHA-256: `2735eda6746b271a09526dbda245c706c7cdb3f9df6bc6cb8e9a6d8ad287e67a` It explicitly preserves provider-favorable alternatives and does not claim that a billing defect has already been proved. ## What success looks like A useful collective result would produce: - a comparable incident index; - clusters by version/model/tier/surface; - reproducible calculations; - documented counterexamples; - a correction log; - a short common provider-side evidence request; - enough portability knowledge that users are not technically trapped; - verified corrections/restoration where records show actual errors. No one speaks for every participant. No majority vote turns a hypothesis into a fact. Corrections are part of the record. Coordination topic: Codex / Pro usage transparency: community evidence and coordination hub Codex Many users report abrupt changes in the usable capacity of Codex and Work plans, especially Pro. The immediate question is not whether one universal bug exists. It is: What did I buy? What consumed it? Can I independently reconcile the two? Do not buy extra credits or rerun expensive failures just to investigate this. When an incident occurs naturally, preserve the evidence you already have: date/timezone, plan, client/version, model/reasoning level, service tier if known, parent/subagent coun… Counterexamples and ordinary-charge explanations are explicitly welcome.

- 2026-10-20T00:22 AhjinSystems https://community.openai.com/t/1409401/930 ::  Could OpenAI consider making promotional global usage resets additive instead of replacing the remaining allowance? For example, if I have 40% of my weekly allowance left, a global reset could add 100 percentage points, bringing me to 140%, capped at 200%. My normal weekly reset date would stay unchanged. Any expiry and treatment of bonus allowance at the next regular reset should be clearly stated. The current approach creates a use-it-or-lose-it incentive: users may rush to consume remaining allowance, enable faster modes or start extra work shortly before a reset. That can encourage spending capacity because it is about to disappear rather than because the work needs it. An additive bonus would let users plan useful work more calmly and receive the full intended benefit regardless of how much allowance remained when the announcement arrived. It might also reduce synchronized bursts before resets, although that would need measurement. This is a suggestion for bounded promotional capacity, not unlimited usage. A clear cap and transparent expiry could keep it predictable for both OpenAI and users. Would this be worth considering for future global resets?

So whilst I’m not clear how you would validate 750 issues before work started, it definitely would help the project stay on track if those 750 issues were the right ones. This leads me to the belief that the people who are most struggling with quota are the ones that are just letting the agents build whatever they like within a very broad definition. If you slow things down and prioritise and shape specific requirements much more carefully, I believe you are far less likely to run out of quota. Just generating an entire slop application with no explicit human prioritisation and taste - well that’s just an ugly inefficient waste of resources and you can’t really complain if you run out of credits, especially if you then have to go back and change a load of stuff in retrospect.
